# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: The *Notice to the Readers* (*ОТВѢТЪ КЪ ЧИТАТЕЛЕМЪ*) is the customary introductory greeting of the Typography of the Stauropegion Institute of Lviv. The text appears on physical leaf p1, bearing the repair and preservation marks of the Patriarchal Liturgical Commission of the UGCC.

[^2]: Church Slavonic year ,ац҃з corresponds numerically to 1907 (,а = 1000, ц = 900, з = 7).

[^3]: An epigraphic formula standard in Ruthenian and Slavic Cyrillic printings since the 16th century (cf. the editions of Ivan Fedorov and the Lviv Brotherhood), entreating clerical readers and chanters to correct typesetting lapses with Christian meekness rather than condemnation.

[^4]: The master title page on leaf p2 demonstrates the historical bilingual title: Church Slavonic *Triod Tsvitnaya* (*ТРІѠ́ДЬ ЦВѢ́ТНАЯ*) glossed by its canonical Greek origin *Pentekostarion* (*ПЕНТИКОСТА́РЇОНЪ*), defined in the vernacular as *Piatdesiatnytsia* (*ПЯТДЕСЯ́ТНИЦЯ*, the Fiftieth Day Book / Pentecostarion).

[^5]: The Church of the Dormition of the Most Holy Mother of God (the "Wallachian" or Dormition Church) in Lviv served from the 16th through the 20th century as the primary temple, institutional heart, and publishing seat of the Stauropegion Brotherhood and Institute.

[^6]: Physical leaf p3 features a full-page architectural plate depicting the Dormition Church complex in Lviv, showcasing Paolo Romanus's Renaissance facade (1591–1629), the Chapel of the Three Hierarchs (1578–1591), and Petro Krasovsky's 66-meter Korniakt Bell Tower (1572–1578).

[^7]: Pope Saint Pius X (Giuseppe Melchiorre Sarto), Supreme Pontiff of the Catholic Church from August 4, 1903, until his death on August 20, 1914. Emperor Franz Joseph I of Austria and Apostolic King of Hungary reigned over the Austro-Hungarian Empire from 1848 to 1916.

[^8]: Servant of God Metropolitan Andrey Count Sheptytsky, OSBM (1865–1944), Metropolitan of Halych, Archbishop of Lviv, and Bishop of Kamianets-Podilskyi from 1900 to 1944. The official curial registry records the approbation on April 22, 1907, under No. 2743.

[^9]: Metropolitan Sheptytsky held statutory dignities within the Cisleithanian Austrian administration, including Privy Councillor (*Geheimer Rat*), Member of the Austrian Imperial House of Lords (*Herrenhaus*), and Deputy Marshal (*Vizelandmarschall*) of the Galician Provincial Diet (*Sejm Krajowy*).

[^10]: Bishop Konstantyn Chekhovych (1847–1915), Ukrainian Greek-Catholic Bishop of the Eparchy of Peremyshl, Sambir, and Sanok from 1896 until his arrest and martyrdom during the Russian occupation of Galicia in 1915.

[^11]: Blessed Hieromartyr Hryhoriy Khomyshyn (1867–1945), Ukrainian Greek-Catholic Bishop of the Eparchy of Stanyslaviv (now Ivano-Frankivsk) from 1904 until his martyrdom in Kyiv's Lukyanivska Prison in 1945; beatified by Pope Saint John Paul II in Lviv on June 27, 2001.

[^12]: Roman Yminsky served as Senior Elder (*Starshyna*) of the Stauropegion Institute in 1907 alongside council members Nicholas Hnylyakevych and Benedict Yanitsky. The editorial redaction was supervised by Father Volodymyr Hurhula, and typographical execution was managed by Michael Reftsio.

[^13]: Codicological Note on the Table of Contents: This 1907 Lviv Stauropegion edition of the Flowery Triodion follows the traditional Galician/Ruthenian recension by incorporating the full liturgical services of the eve of Lazarus Saturday (Friday of Palm Week), Lazarus Saturday, Palm Sunday, and Great and Holy Passion Week (pages 1–197), followed on page 198 by the Pentecostarion proper (*Начало Пентикостаріа сирѣчь Пятдесятницы*).

[^14]: The Feast of the Most Holy Mystery of the Eucharist (Corpus Christi, instituted for the Ruthenian Church at the Synod of Zamość in 1720) and the Feast of the Compassion of the Most Holy Theotokos were standard proper feasts included in Galician editions of the Pentecostarion following All Saints Sunday.

[^15]: Iconographic note on leaf p8: The engraving of the Dormition depicts the ancient tradition of the Archangel Michael intervening when the Jewish priest Jephonias (or Athonios) insolently reached out to topple the bier of the Mother of God during her burial procession in Gethsemane; upon repenting and confessing Christ, his severed hands were miraculously restored.

[^16]: Codicological Landmark Note: In this 1907 Lviv Stauropegion edition of the Flowery Triodion (*Triod Tsvitnaya* / Pentecostarion), the primary liturgical text opens on printed page 1 (physical leaf p9) with Friday of Palm Week (the eve of Lazarus Saturday), continuing through Lazarus Saturday, Palm Sunday, and Great and Holy Passion Week before reaching Holy Pascha on page 198 (physical leaf p206). In accordance with the publication specification and Master Translation Standard MTS-1, Part I opens here at the inception of the liturgical text.

[^17]: Emperor Leo VI the Wise (866–912), Byzantine emperor and hymnographer, authored the series of Idiomela (*samoglasny*) for Lazarus Saturday chanting the resurrection of Lazarus as the pledge of the universal resurrection.

[^18]: The vesperal reading on Friday of the 6th Week of the Great Fast (eve of Lazarus Saturday) is from Genesis 49:33–50:26 according to the Septuagint (LXX) text, recounting the death and embalming of Jacob in Egypt and Joseph's oath to bury him in the land of Canaan.

[^19]: The second reading at Vespers on Friday of Palm Week is the Praise of the Valiant Woman (LXX Proverbs 31:8–31 / MT 31:10–31), opening with the counsel to open one's mouth in defense of the poor and weak, prefiguring the pious women (Martha and Mary) who served the Lord and received Lazarus back from the tomb.

[^20]: Rubrical note on leaf p12: From this day forward (Friday of Palm Week), neither the Martyrikon (*Мꙋ́чениченъ*), nor the Theotokion (*Бг҃оро́диченъ*), nor the Octoechos (*Ѻ҆смогла́сникъ*) is chanted until the Saturday of Saint Thomas (Antipascha), marking the complete cessation of the weekly Octoechos cycle for the duration of Passion Week and Bright Week.

[^21]: The liturgical rubric notes the divergence between the general Byzantine monastic practice appointing Great Compline (*Повече́рїе вели́кое*) and the specific Ruthenian regional usage established at the Synod of Lviv (1891), which appointed Small Compline (*Повече́рїе ма́лое*) together with the Canon of Saint Andrew of Crete for the eve of Lazarus Saturday.

[^22]: Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), Archbishop of Gortyna and author of the Great Canon of Repentance, is surnamed "the Hierosolymite" (*І҆еросолими́тъ*) in reference to his monastic tonsure and early ascetic formation at the Lavra of Saint Sabbas the Sanctified in Jerusalem.

[^23]: Lazarus Saturday (*Суббѡ́та ст҃а́гѡ и҆ пра́веднагѡ Ла́зарѧ*) commemorates the raising of the four-days-dead Lazarus of Bethany (John 11:1–45) by Our Lord Jesus Christ as the solemn pledge and prefiguration of the universal resurrection before His own voluntary Passion and death.

[^24]: The Midnight Office rubrics for Lazarus Saturday prescribe the festal troparion (*Confirming the common resurrection...*) and kontakion (*Christ, the joy of all...*), omitting the customary penitential prayers for the departed (*Помянѝ, Го́споди...*).

[^25]: The chanting of the Resurrectional Troparia of the Blameless (*Еѵлогїта́рїа* / *А҆́ггельскїй собо́ръ*, "Blessed art Thou, O Lord: teach me Thy statutes") on a Saturday is a unique liturgical occurrence found only on Lazarus Saturday and Great and Holy Saturday, testifying to the intensely resurrectional character of the day.

[^26]: The Matins Gospel is omitted on Lazarus Saturday except in a church dedicated to Saint Lazarus. The appointed patristic reading consists of the homiletic commentary on John 11 by Saint John Chrysostom (*Homilies 62 and 63 on the Gospel of John*, designated in the Slavonic text as chapters 33 and 34 of the commentary).

[^27]: The Matins canon combines two compositions: the first by Saint Theophanes the Branded (*Theophanes Graptos*, ca. 778–845), Metropolitan of Nicaea; the second by Saint John Damascene. From Ode 6, the office incorporates the four-ode canon (*четверопѣ́снецъ*) of Saint Cosmas of Maiuma.

[^28]: "John the Monk" (*І҆ѡа́ннъ мона́хъ*) is the standard liturgical designation for Saint John of Damascus (Damascene, ca. 676–749), monk of the Lavra of Saint Sabbas and Doctor of the Church.

[^29]: The Sessional Hymn model *Tone 8, Podoben: "The Wisdom..." (Премꙋ́дрости)* is the celebrated poetic incipit *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον ἐν σῇ γαστρὶ συλλαβοῦσα* ("Having conceived the Wisdom and the Word in thy womb"), attributed to Saint Theodore the Studite or Saint John Damascene, widely utilized across the Triodion and Pentecostarion for festive sessional compositions.

[^30]: Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), foster brother of Saint John Damascene, composed the four-ode canon (*четверопѣ́снецъ*) spanning Odes 6 through 9, which forms the concluding portion of the Matins canon for Lazarus Saturday.

[^31]: The Kontakion model *Tone 2, Podoben: "Seeking the things on high" (Вы́шнихъ и҆щѧ̀)* (*Τὰ ἄνω ζητῶν*) is Saint Romanos the Melodist's classical prooimion model, here applied to Lazarus Saturday to proclaim Christ as the universal Joy, Truth, and Resurrection of all.

[^32]: The liturgical Synaxarion for Lazarus Saturday from the historical compendium of Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335). It synthesizes patristic traditions concerning Lazarus's life after his resurrection, including his flight to Kition in Cyprus to escape the Sanhedrin, his thirty-year episcopate, the hand-woven omophorion gifted him by the Theotokos, and the translation of his relics to Constantinople under Emperor Leo VI the Wise (reigned 886–912) in 899.

[^33]: In place of the Trisagion (*Свѧты́й Бж҃е*), the baptismal hymn from Galatians 3:27 (*"As many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ. Alleluia"*) is sung at the Divine Liturgy on Lazarus Saturday. In the ancient cathedral usage of Constantinople and Jerusalem, this day was one of the primary baptismal vigils of the liturgical year, when catechumens were solemnly baptized into Christ prior to Holy Week.

[^34]: Monastic dietary mitigation on Lazarus Saturday: the Great Fast rule of xerophagy is mitigated to permit cooked food with olive oil (*варе́нїемъ со є҆ле́емъ*) and wine. Furthermore, the consumption of fish roe/caviar (*и҆кра̀*) is explicitly permitted up to three ounces (*трѝ ѻ҆́нгїи*), while whole fish remains strictly prohibited until Palm Sunday.

[^35]: The rubrical statute for a parish or monastery temple dedicated to Saint Lazarus directs the chanting of the Polyeleos at Matins, the First Antiphon of the Anavathmoi (Hymns of Ascent) in Tone 4, and the full patristic reading of the Life of Saint Lazarus composed by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos.

[^36]: Small Vespers on the Eve of Palm Sunday (*на ма́лѣй вече́рни въ сꙋббѡ́тꙋ ва́їй ве́чера*) is celebrated before sunset in monastic communities, preceding the solemn festal Great Vespers. Its stichera anticipate the Entry of the Lord into Jerusalem riding upon the foal of an ass.

[^37]: Great Vespers on Saturday Evening begins the Great Feast of the Palm Sunday of the Lord's Entry into Jerusalem (*Вхо́дъ Гдⷭ҇ень въ І҆ерꙋсали́мъ* / *Недѣ́лѧ Ва́їй*), one of the Twelve Great Feasts (*Dodekaorton*). The opening sticheron, *"Today the grace of the Holy Spirit hath gathered us together..."* (*Дне́сь бл҃года́ть ст҃а́гѡ Дх҃а на́съ собра̀*), is one of the most renowned processional idiomela in Byzantine hymnography.

[^38]: The Old Testament Paremias at Palm Sunday Great Vespers: Genesis 49:1-2, 8-12 (the Patriarch Jacob's dying blessing upon Judah, prophesying the perpetual royal sceptre, the expectation of the nations, and the King binding His foal unto the vine and washing His garments in the blood of the grape); Zephaniah 3:14-19 (the joyous advent of the King of Israel in Zion, removing judgment and casting out enemies); and Zechariah 9:9-15 (the messianic prophecy of the meek King entering Jerusalem mounted upon the foal of an ass, cutting off chariot and war-bow, and establishing universal peace).

[^39]: The festal dismissal troparia of Palm Sunday: The primary troparion, *Tone 1: "Giving assurance of the general resurrection before Thy Passion, Thou didst raise Lazarus from the dead, O Christ our God; therefore we also, like the children, carrying the tokens of victory, cry aloud to Thee, the Vanquisher of death: Hosanna in the highest! Blessed is He that comes in the name of the Lord"* (chanted twice), and the baptismal troparion, *Tone 4: "Having been buried with Thee through baptism, O Christ our God, we have been made worthy of immortal life through Thy Resurrection; and chanting we cry aloud: Hosanna in the highest! Blessed is He that comes in the name of the Lord"* (chanted once).

[^40]: Physical Leaf p26 (fol. 21r).

[^41]: The solemn Prayer for the Blessing of Palms (*Моли́тва на бл҃гослове́нїе ва́їа*) recited by the priest at Matins after the chanting of the festal Gospel and Psalm 50, followed by the ceremonial distribution of blessed palms and willow branches (*ва́їа и҆ вѣ́тви древе́съ*) to the clergy and faithful.

[^42]: The Canon of Palm Sunday Matins composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752). The Greek acrostic formed by the initial letters of the troparia across Odes 1 through 9 reads: *Ὡσαννὰ Χριστὸς εὐλογημένος Θεός* ("Hosanna, Christ is blessed God").

[^43]: The Ikos of Palm Sunday draws a theological contrast between the slaughter of the Innocents in Bethlehem at Christ's Nativity and His voluntary self-offering upon the Cross for all humanity, both young and old.

[^44]: The Synaxarion for Palm Sunday (*Вхо́дъ Гдⷭ҇ень въ І҆ерꙋсали́мъ* / *Ἡ Κυριακὴ τῶν Βαΐων*) by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335). It expounds the historical circumstances of the Lord's royal entry into Jerusalem six days before the Passover, the typology of the palms (*ва́їа*) as emblems of victory over death, and the foal of an ass as the prefiguration of the Gentile nations called from idolatrous irrationality into the Church of God.

[^45]: At Ode 9 of the Matins Canon on Palm Sunday, the biblical canticle of the Theotokos (*Magnificat* / *Честнѣ́йшꙋю*) is omitted in accordance with the rubrics for Master Feasts of the Lord (*Владычніи праздники*), proceeding immediately to the festal heirmos.

[^46]: The Festive Antiphons of Palm Sunday at the Divine Liturgy are taken from Psalms 114, 115, and 117 (LXX). Each antiphon features its own proper festal refrain celebrating Christ's entry upon the foal.

[^47]: The Festal Entrance Hymn (*Входное*) at the Little Entrance of the Divine Liturgy is taken from Psalm 117:26-27a: *"Blessed is He that comes in the name of the Lord; we have blessed you out of the house of the Lord; God is the Lord, and hath appeared unto us."*

[^48]: Monastic dietary mitigation on Palm Sunday: Although occurring within Great Lent, because the Entry of the Lord into Jerusalem is one of the Twelve Great Feasts, the typikon mitigates the strict Lenten fast to permit the consumption of fish, wine, and oil (*ꙗ҆ди́мъ ры́бы, и҆ вїно̀ пїе́мъ*).

[^49]: Palm Sunday Evening Vespers opens Great and Holy Week and marks the liturgical transition from the festal triumph of the Entry into Jerusalem to the solemn, sober anticipation of the Lord's Sufferings and the Bridegroom Matins services (*Служба Жениха*).

[^50]: Palm Sunday Evening dismissal order: The penitential conclusion of Palm Sunday Vespers marks the formal entrance into the severe fast and solemnity of Great and Holy Passion Week (*Страстная Седмица*), reintroducing the great prostrations with the prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian and the penitential troparia (*"Rejoice, O Virgin Theotokos"* / *Богоро́дице Дѣ́во ра́дꙋйсѧ*).

[^51]: The Compline Tri-ode of Saint Andrew of Crete: Throughout Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, Small Compline features a three-ode canon (tri-ode / *трипѣ́снецъ*) composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), Archbishop of Gortyna, meditating on the impending voluntary Passion of Christ and the typological figures of the day (Joseph the All-Comely and the Barren Fig Tree).

[^52]: The reading of the Psalter during Passion Week: In accordance with ancient Studite and Sabaite typika, the entire Psalter (all 20 kathismata) is read once through between Monday and Wednesday noon (three kathismata at Matins, and one each at the Third and Sixth Hours), ceasing completely after the Presanctified Divine Liturgy of Great Wednesday.

[^53]: The 17th Kathisma (Psalm 118 LXX / *Непоро́чны*) is reserved for Great and Holy Saturday Matins at the Burial of Christ (the Lamentations / *Погребе́нїе Хрїсто́во*), while Kathisma 18 (the Gradual Psalms 119–133 LXX) is sung at Vespers throughout Great Lent and Holy Week.

[^54]: The Bridegroom Matins (*Служба Жениха*) of Great and Holy Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday: Celebrated by anticipation on the preceding evening in parish and monastic practice. The solemn Troparion *"Behold, the Bridegroom comes at midnight"* (*Се́ Жени́хъ гряде́тъ въ полꙋ́нощи*) derives from the Parable of the Ten Virgins (Matthew 25:1–13) and serves as the thematic anthem of the opening days of Passion Week.

[^55]: Patristic readings at Bridegroom Matins: The appointed homiletic commentaries of Saint John Chrysostom on the Gospel of Matthew (specifically Homilies 67 and 68, expounding the withering of the barren fig tree and the cleansing of the Temple).

[^56]: Saint John Chrysostom's Homily on the Withered Fig Tree (*Сло́во о и҆зсо́хшей смоко́вницѣ*), examining the mystical significance of the curse pronounced upon the leafy, fruitless tree as a prophetic judgment upon the formalist hypocrisy of the Old Covenant leadership.

[^57]: Saint John Damascene's Homily on the Withered Fig Tree and the Parable of the Wicked Vinedressers (Matthew 21:33–44).

[^58]: The Matins Tri-ode of Great Monday composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), bearing the Greek alphabetical acrostic *Τῇ δευτέρᾳ* ("On the Second Day," i.e., Monday), spanning Odes 1, 8, and 9.

[^59]: The Synaxarion for Great and Holy Monday by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335), commemorating two foundational types: the Patriarch Joseph the All-Comely (*І҆ѡ́сифъ прекра́сный*) as a prefiguration of Christ's betrayal, descent into the pit, and exaltation as Ruler and Giver of the Bread of Life; and the cursed and withered fig tree (*и҆зсо́хшая смоко́вница*) as an emblem of spiritual barrenness and hypocrisy.

[^60]: Saint Isidore of Pelusium (ca. 360–435 or 450), ascetic abbot and prolific theologian in Egypt, who in his *Epistles* (Book I, Letter 153) recorded the ancient patristic tradition identifying the Tree of the Knowledge of Good and Evil in Paradise as a fig tree, whose leaves Adam and Eve used to sew aprons after the Fall.

[^61]: The celebrated Exapostilarion (Hymn of Light / *Свѣти́ленъ*) of Passion Week, *"Thy bridal chamber I behold adorned, O my Savior..."* (*Черто́гъ тво́й ви́ждꙋ, Спа́се мо́й*), chanted thrice by a soloist in the centre of the church while the congregation kneels, expressing the soul's yearning for the heavenly bridal banquet and its acute unworthiness.

[^62]: The proper dismissal formula for Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday: *"The Lord, going unto His voluntary Passion for our salvation, Christ our true God..."* (*Гряды́й Госпо́дь на во́льнꙋю Стра́сть, на́шегѡ ра́ди спасе́нїя, Христо́съ и҆́стинный Бо́гъ на́шъ...*).

[^63]: The continuous reading of the Four Gospels (*Четвероева́нгелїе*) during the Hours of Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday: The entirety of the four Evangelists (Matthew, Mark, and Luke complete; John read up to 13:31) is solemnly proclaimed from an adorned analogion positioned in the nave, completing the full Gospel witness before the commemorative enactment of the Lord's Passion.

[^64]: The Sixth Hour prophecy on Great Monday: Ezekiel 1:1–20, recounting the Prophet Ezekiel's vision of the divine chariot-throne (*Merkavah*) by the river Chebar, with the four winged living creatures (the Tetramorph) bearing the likenesses of a man, a lion, an ox, and an eagle—patristically interpreted by Saint Irenaeus of Lyons and Saint Jerome as the four Evangelists.

[^65]: Codicological Note on the Passion Gospels Rubric: The text notes the liturgical distribution of the Four Gospels across the first three days of Great Week, preparing for the proclamation of the Twelve Passion Gospels at Great Thursday Matins.

[^66]: The continuous reading of the Four Gospels (*Четвероева́нгелїе*) during Holy Week: Matthew, Mark, and Luke are read in their entirety, while John is read up to the discourse on the Passion (John 13:31). The readings are distributed across nine sessions during the Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours of Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, concluding at the Ninth Hour of Great Wednesday before the Presanctified Divine Liturgy.

[^67]: The liturgical order of the Ninth Hour on Great Monday through Wednesday omits the customary kathisma psalmody (*стїхосло́вїе*). The service proceeds directly from the three fixed Psalms to the Troparion of the Ninth Hour, followed by the Gospel proclamation from the adorned analogion in the center of the nave.

[^68]: At Great Monday Presanctified Vespers, the appointed stichera on *Lord, I have cried* reprise the solemn idiomel hymns of Monday Matins (*"The Lord, going unto His voluntary Passion..."*), emphasizing the journey toward Jerusalem and the voluntary character of Christ's sacrifice.

[^69]: The Old Testament reading from Exodus 1:1–20 at Presanctified Vespers recounts the enslavement and affliction of Israel under Pharaoh in Egypt. In patristic typology, Pharaoh figures the devil, Egypt represents the tyranny of sin, and the brick-making servitude prefigures humanity's spiritual bondage prior to Christ's redemption.

[^70]: Heliopolis (On / *Ὢν, ἥ ἐστιν Ἡλιούπολις*): Exodus 1:11 in the Septuagint explicitly witnesses three fortified treasure cities built for Pharaoh—Pithom, Raamses, and On (Heliopolis)—whereas the Masoretic Text names only Pithom and Raamses.

[^71]: The Old Testament reading from Job 1:1–12 at Presanctified Vespers presents the righteous Job as an innocent sufferer whose sudden and severe trials prefigure the unmerited sufferings, passion, and eventual vindication of Christ.

[^72]: The ancient Studite-Sabaite fasting rule prescribes xerophagy (*сꙋхоꙗде́нїе*—eating only uncooked or dried food, bread, water, and raw or salt-preserved vegetables without oil or wine) for the first three days of Passion Week, mirroring the severe ascetic discipline of the First Week of Great Lent.

[^73]: Canon 89 of the Sixth Ecumenical Council (In Trullo, 692) directs the faithful to spend the days of the saving Passion in fasting, prayer, and contrition of heart, terminating the Paschal fast at midnight of Great Saturday, in accordance with the Gospel testimonies of Matthew (28:1) and Luke (24:1).

[^74]: Great Compline on Holy Monday evening features a three-ode canon (tri-ode / *трипѣ́снецъ*) by Saint Andrew of Crete (Odes 2, 8, and 9), meditating on Christ's eschatological discourse on the Mount of Olives (Matthew 24–25).

[^75]: The Parable of the Millstones (*же́рновная при́тча*) refers to Matthew 24:41 and Luke 17:35: "Two women shall be grinding at the mill; the one shall be taken, and the other left," allegorically interpreted by the Church Fathers as distinguishing those who work with spiritual vigilance from those who labor in earthly distraction.

[^76]: Matins of Great and Holy Tuesday centers on the Parable of the Ten Virgins (Matthew 25:1–13) and the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14–30), calling the soul to active vigilance, spiritual sobriety, and works of mercy before the imminent arrival of the Heavenly Bridegroom.

[^77]: Codicological cross-reference to printed folio 37 (Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Folio 37 + 5), where the Troparion of the Bridegroom (*"Behold, the Bridegroom comes at midnight..."* / *Се́ Жени́хъ гряде́тъ*) is given in full at the opening of Passion Week on Great Monday.

[^78]: The Synaxarion for Great and Holy Tuesday, composed by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335), expounds the Parable of the Ten Virgins, demonstrating that bodily virginity and ascetic rigor without the oil of mercy, compassion, and active charity cannot open the gates of the heavenly bridal chamber.

[^79]: Patristic teaching on virginity and almsgiving: The synaxarist invokes Saint Isidore of Pelusium and Saint John Chrysostom in teaching that nothing is more disgraceful than virginity overcome by avarice or stinginess toward the poor.

[^80]: The Matins Di-ode of Great Tuesday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), consists of Odes 8 and 9 and carries the Greek alphabetical acrostic *Τρίτῃ τε* ("And on the Third Day"), celebrating the adornment of spiritual lamps with the oil of charity.

[^81]: The solemn Exapostilarion (Hymn of Light / *Свѣти́ленъ*) of Passion Week, *"Thy bridal chamber I behold adorned, O my Savior..."* (*Черто́гъ тво́й ви́ждꙋ, Спа́се мо́й*), chanted thrice at the conclusion of the canon while the church is in darkness or lit only by candlelight.

[^82]: The Sixth Hour prophecy on Great Tuesday: Ezekiel 1:21–28, continuing the Prophet Ezekiel's vision of the chariot-throne (*Merkavah*), the crystalline firmament, the sound of wings like the Almighty (*El Shaddai*), and the sapphire throne bearing the appearance of the Glory of the Lord.

[^83]: The conclusion of the Sixth Hour prophecy on Great Tuesday: Ezekiel 1:21–28, recounting the vision of the four living creatures, the crystalline firmament, the sound of the wings like the voice of the Almighty (*Shaddai*), the sapphire throne, and the appearance of the likeness of the Glory of the Lord.

[^84]: The liturgical order of the Ninth Hour on Great Tuesday omits the customary kathisma psalmody (*стїхосло́вїе*). The service proceeds directly to the Troparion of the Ninth Hour and the Gospel reading, concluding with the Beatitudes (*Блаже́нни*) and prostrations before the Presanctified Divine Liturgy.

[^85]: The first Old Testament reading at Great Tuesday Presanctified Vespers: Exodus 2:5–10, recounting the deliverance of the infant Moses from the waters of the Nile by Pharaoh's daughter, prefiguring Christ's deliverance from the wrath of earthly rulers and the salvation of mankind through the waters of Baptism.

[^86]: The second Old Testament reading at Great Tuesday Presanctified Vespers: Job 1:13–22, describing the sudden and total loss of Job's possessions, servants, and children, and his blameless submission to divine providence (*"The Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord"*).

[^87]: The Gospel reading at Great Tuesday Presanctified Vespers: Matthew 26:6–16 (section 120), recounting the anointing of Christ by the woman with the alabaster box of costly spikenard in the house of Simon the leper in Bethany, contrasted with Judas Iscariot's covenant with the chief priests to betray Him for thirty pieces of silver.

[^88]: Great Compline on Great Tuesday evening features a tri-ode (*трипѣ́снецъ*) composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), consisting of Odes 3, 8, and 9, meditating on spiritual vigilance, the suddenness of the Bridegroom's return, and the repentant anointing by the sinful woman.

[^89]: The solemn Sessional Hymn at Great Compline, Tone 1, Podoben: "The angelic choir" (*Ли́къ а҆́гг҃льскїй*), contemplating the lawless Sanhedrin—Annas, Caiaphas, Herod, and Pilate—gathering in hatred to examine and condemn the Long-suffering Lord of all.

[^90]: Matins of Holy and Great Wednesday centers on two diametrically opposed figures: the repentant harlot who poured out costly myrrh upon the Lord's feet to obtain the remission of her sins, and Judas Iscariot who, blinded by avarice, sold his Master to the chief priests for thirty pieces of silver.

[^91]: Codicological cross-reference to printed folio 37 (Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Folio 37 + 5), where the solemn Troparion of the Bridegroom (*"Behold, the Bridegroom comes at midnight..."* / *Се́ Жени́хъ гряде́тъ*) is given in full at the opening of Passion Week on Great Monday.

[^92]: The Matins Gospel of Holy and Great Wednesday: John 12:17–50 (sections 41 to 43), recounting the testimony of the crowds regarding the raising of Lazarus, the Greeks seeking Jesus, the Father's voice from heaven, Christ's prophecy of being lifted up upon the Cross, and His solemn warning to walk while they have the Light.

[^93]: The Matins Tri-ode of Holy and Great Wednesday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), consisting of Odes 3, 8, and 9, bearing the Greek acrostic *Τετράδι ψάλλω* ("On Wednesday I sing" / *Тетра́ди ѱалῶ*).

[^94]: The Kontakion of Holy and Great Wednesday, Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross" (*Вознесы́йсѧ*), voicing the soul's profound sorrow for having exceeded the harlot in sinfulness without offering tears, yet begging for forgiveness through silent supplication.

[^95]: The Synaxarion of Holy and Great Wednesday, composed by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335): An extensive patristic treatise harmonizing the differing Gospel accounts of the women who anointed the Lord with myrrh.

[^96]: Patristic harmonization of Saint John Chrysostom (Homily 80 on Matthew): Chrysostom teaches that there were two distinct women: the repentant sinful woman of Matthew, Mark, and Luke, and Mary the sister of Lazarus in John. Nikephoros Kallistos also discusses the opinion of other patristic commentators that three distinct anointings occurred: the repentant sinner in Galilee (Luke 7), Mary of Bethany six days before Pascha (John 12), and another woman in the house of Simon the leper two days before Pascha (Matthew 26, Mark 14).

[^97]: Epiphanius of Salamis (*De Mensuris et Ponderibus*) and patristic realia: The alabaster box (*а҆лава́стръ*, Greek *ἀλάβαστρον*) was a narrow-necked glass or stone vial without handles (*вѵ́кїонъ*, Greek *βικίον*), designed to preserve volatile ointments; *nard pistic* (*на́рдъ пістико́съ*, Greek *νάρδος πιστική*) denotes genuine, pure, unadulterated spikenard.

[^98]: Patristic tradition concerning the Upper Room (*го́рница по́стлана*): Xanthopoulos distinguishes the two suburban Bethany banquets from the Last Supper, noting that the furnished upper room inside Jerusalem on Mount Zion served not only for the Mystical Supper, but also as the refuge of the Apostles after the Crucifixion, the site of the post-Resurrection appearances, the touching of Saint Thomas, and the Descent of the Holy Spirit at Pentecost.

[^99]: The Praises (Lauds) of Holy and Great Wednesday Matins: Four idiomel stichera in Tone 1, vividly contrasting the weeping harlot untying her hair and washing the Savior's feet with Judas Iscariot plotting betrayal and calculating thirty pieces of silver.

[^100]: The Aposticha of Holy and Great Wednesday Matins: Solemn idiomel stichera in Tone 6, meditating on Christ's boundless condescension in dining at the house of the Pharisee and accepting the sinful woman's tears, while Judas extends his hands to receive the price of blood.

[^101]: The celebrated Idiomel of Saint Kassiani the Nun (Kassia, ca. 810–865), chanted at Holy Wednesday Matins and Presanctified Vespers: Tone 8, *"Lord, the woman who had fallen into many sins..."* (*Господи, яже во многїя грѣхи впадшая жена*). One of the most famous liturgical compositions of the Byzantine and Slavonic traditions, it poignantly portrays the weeping sinful woman anointing Christ's feet in anticipation of His burial, contrasting her repentance with Eve's terror in the Garden of Eden.

[^102]: Codicological cross-reference to Great Monday (Physical Leaf p46 = Printed Folio 41 + 5), where the full liturgical order of the Hours (Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours) with kathisma psalmody, Gospel readings, and the Prayer of Saint Ephraim the Syrian is detailed in full.

[^103]: The Sixth Hour Old Testament reading of Holy and Great Wednesday: Ezekiel 2:1–3:3, recounting the prophet Ezekiel's divine vocation to preach repentance to the rebellious and stiff-hearted house of Israel, and his eating of the scroll inscribed with lamentation and woe, which tasted in his mouth sweet as honey.

[^104]: The Gospel reading at the Sixth Hour on Holy and Great Wednesday: John 12:17–50, concluding the third of the four daily Gospel lessons of Passion Week.

[^105]: The Ninth Hour of Holy and Great Wednesday marks the definitive conclusion of the reading of the Four Gospels (*четвероевангелїе*) that began on Great Monday. The final reading covers the Passion narrative up to the betrayal.

[^106]: The concluding monastic prayer of the Ninth Hour: *"O Master of great mercy, Lord Jesus Christ our God, Who through Thine ineffable love for mankind..."* (*Владыко многомилостиве*), during which the entire monastic brotherhood remains prostrated on the ground, culminating in the solemn mutual Rite of Forgiveness (*Чинъ прощенїя*) that formally marks the conclusion of the Forty Days of Great Lent.

[^107]: The reciprocal monastic forgiveness formula: each monk approaches the Superior and fellow brethren two by two, bowing to the earth and asking: *"Forgive me, holy father, a sinner, wherein I have sinned against thee in word, deed, or thought."*

[^108]: Codicological and typikon distinction: In monasteries, the brethren disperse to their cells after the mutual forgiveness to maintain absolute prayerful silence until Vespers; in cathedral and parish churches, however, the faithful remain assembled as the bells ring a festive chime (*трезвонъ*) signaling the immediate transition to Presanctified Vespers.

[^109]: The ten stichera on *"Lord, I have cried"* at Presanctified Vespers of Holy and Great Wednesday: five idiomel stichera repeated to make ten (including repetitions of the Matins Praises and Aposticha), focusing intensely on the moral and spiritual contrast between the repentant sinful woman who offered costly spikenard and Judas Iscariot who bartered his Master for thirty pieces of silver.

[^110]: The solemn repetition of Saint Kassiani's Idiomel at Vespers, Tone 8, serving as the Doxastikon (*Слава, и нынѣ*) during the Entrance with the Holy Gospel at the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.

[^111]: The Entrance with the Holy Gospel (*Входъ со Евангелїемъ*) at Presanctified Vespers: prescribed on Holy Wednesday because of the solemn Gospel reading (Matthew 26:6–16) appointed for the Presanctified Liturgy.

[^112]: The first Old Testament reading at Holy Wednesday Presanctified Vespers: Exodus 2:11–22, recounting Moses slaying the Egyptian taskmaster who was beating a Hebrew, his flight into Midian, his defense of the daughters of Jethro (Reuel) at the well, and his marriage to Zipporah.

[^113]: The second Old Testament reading at Holy Wednesday Presanctified Vespers: Job 2:1–10 (spanning physical leaves p75 and p76), recounting the second celestial assembly, Satan obtaining permission to afflict Job's flesh with grievous boils, and Job sitting upon the ashes outside the city.

[^114]: Septuagintal and recensional note on Job 2:9 LXX: The Slavonic printed text preserves the extended Greek Septuagint reading of the desperate lamentation of Job's wife (*"How long wilt thou endure... speak some word against the Lord, and die!"*), which is absent from the Masoretic Hebrew text. The bracketed clause `[и не даде безумїя Богу]` represents the canonical LXX ending of Job 2:10 (*"and he did not charge God foolishly"* / *οὐδὲ ἔδωκεν ἀφροσύνην τῷ Θεῷ*).

[^115]: The Presanctified Gospel of Holy and Great Wednesday: Matthew 26:6–16 (section 150), detailing the anointing of Christ by the woman in the house of Simon the leper in Bethany, and Judas Iscariot negotiating His betrayal with the chief priests for thirty pieces of silver.

[^116]: The liturgical cessation of prostrations: The three great prostrations made after the transfer of the Presanctified Gifts mark the solemn cessation of all full prostrations (*метанія великїя*) within the church temple until the Kneeling Prayers of Pentecost Vespers (with the sole exception of prostrations before the Holy Epitaphios and the Cross). In private cell devotions, prostrations continue until Great Friday.

[^117]: Small Compline on Holy Wednesday evening features a tri-ode (*трипѣснецъ*) composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), consisting of Odes 4, 8, and 9, dedicated to the mysteries of Great Thursday: the preparation of the Upper Room, the Mystical Supper, the Washing of the Feet, and the contrast between the true disciples and the betrayal of Judas.

[^118]: The Sessional Hymn at Compline, Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou hast appeared today" (*Явился еси днесь* / *Ἐπεφάνης σήμερον*), meditating on the institution of the Holy Eucharist as the mystical prefiguration of Christ's sacrifice on the Cross.

[^119]: Typikon rubric on the Midnight Office: From Holy Wednesday evening onward, the Midnight Office (*Полунощница*) is entirely omitted from public church celebrations throughout Passion Week and Bright Week, resuming only on the Sunday of Saint Thomas (Antipascha).

[^120]: Codicological and iconographic description of the full-width woodcut engraving on fol. 75r (Physical Leaf p80) depicting the Washing of the Feet (Mandatum / *Умовенїе ногъ*): Christ, kneeling and girded with a linen towel, washes the feet of Saint Peter, while the other Apostles sit or stand around the table in the Upper Room of Mount Sion.

[^121]: The nocturnal striking of the semantron (*параекклисїархъ клеплетъ*) in the seventh hour of the night (approximately 1:00 AM) marks the ancient monastic schedule for Matins of Holy and Great Thursday.

[^122]: The solemn Troparion of Holy and Great Thursday Matins, Tone 8: *"When the glorious disciples were enlightened at the washing of the Supper..."* (*Егда славнїи ученицы на умовенїи Вечери просвѣщахуся*), chanted thrice with Alleluia instead of "God is the Lord," summarizing the central themes of the Day: the illumination of the Apostles, the dark madness of Judas's avarice, and Christ's righteous judgment.

[^123]: The Matins Gospel of Holy and Great Thursday: Luke 22:1–39 (beginning in section 108 and concluding within section 109), recounting the preparation of the Passover meal, the institution of the Holy Eucharist, Christ's prophecy of Peter's denial, and His departure to the Mount of Olives.

[^124]: The Canon of Holy and Great Thursday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), set in Tone 6. The 1907 Lviv edition preserves the original Greek iambic acrostic: *Τῇ μακρᾷ πέμπτῃ μακρὸν ὕμνον ᾄδω* (*Тѝ макра̀ пе́мити макро́нъ Ѵ҆́мнонъ ᾄδᾄδω*, literally: "On Great Thursday I sing a long hymn"), rendered into Church Slavonic as *"Въ великїй Четвертокъ долгую пѣснь пою."*

[^125]: The Canon of Holy and Great Thursday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), set in Tone 6. The 1907 Lviv edition features an acrostic whose Greek original (*Τῇ μακρᾷ πέμπτῃ μακρὸν ὕμνον ᾄδω*, "On Great Thursday I sing a long hymn") is marked in the margin letter by letter. The Heirmos of Ode 1 draws a classical typological parallel between the miraculous dividing of the Red Sea by Moses' rod (Exodus 14) and Christ's voluntary Passion, through which believers pass unharmed while death and corruption are overwhelmed.

[^126]: An explicit allusion to Proverbs 9:1: *"Wisdom hath builded Her house, She hath hewn out Her seven pillars; She hath killed Her beasts; She hath mingled Her wine; She hath also furnished Her table."* The Church Fathers universally interpret Holy Wisdom (*Премудрость Божия* / *Ἁγία Σοφία*) as the pre-eternal Word of God, Who took flesh in the womb of the Virgin Mary and prepared the Eucharistic banquet of the Mystical Supper.

[^127]: The Heirmos of Ode 3 meditates on the Kenosis (self-emptying) of God the Word (Philippians 2:7), Who, being rich and impassible, impoverished Himself to unite fallen humanity to the divine nature, offering Himself as the true Paschal Lamb before His historical immolation on the Cross.

[^128]: The Heirmos of Ode 4 takes its thematic imagery from the prophetic prayer of Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:2 LXX), perceiving the divine economy of salvation as the manifestation of the Father's steadfast, unshakeable love in delivering His Only-begotten Son as the propitiation for the sins of the cosmos.

[^129]: A poetic meditation upon Christ's eschatological saying recorded in the Synoptic Gospels (Matthew 26:29; Mark 14:25; Luke 22:18): *"I will not drink henceforth of this fruit of the vine, until that day when I drink it new with you in My Father's Kingdom."* The troparion interprets this "new drink" as the eternal communion of the saints in the Kingdom of God, based upon the Patristic doctrine of theosis (deification): *"for as God I shall be with you who are gods"* (alluding to Psalm 81:6 LXX and John 10:34–35).

[^130]: The Heirmos of Ode 5 celebrates the Sacred Washing of the Feet (the Mandatum, John 13:4–15), linking the cleansing of the Apostles' feet with the prophecy of Isaiah 52:7 LXX: *"How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that brings good tidings, that publishes peace."*

[^131]: The Heirmos of Ode 6 takes its imagery from Jonah's prayer from the belly of the sea monster (Jonah 2:2–7 LXX), the canonical type of Christ's descent into Hades and third-day Resurrection.

[^132]: The Kontakion of Holy and Great Thursday, composed by Saint Romanos the Melodist (fl. early 6th century), set in Tone 2, Podoben: *"Seeking the highest"* (*Вы́шнихъ и҆щя̀* / *Τὰ ἄνω ζητῶν*). It contrasts Judas receiving the Eucharistic Bread with his hands while covertly extending those same hands to receive the blood-money of the thirty pieces of silver.

[^133]: The opening verse of the Synaxarion poetically links the Washing of the Feet at the Mystical Supper with the Protoevangelium of Genesis 3:15: the heel of the New Adam trampling upon the serpent's delusion in the Garden of Eden.

[^134]: The third Synaxarion verse commemorates Christ's agony and bloody sweat in Gethsemane (Luke 22:44: *"And being in an agony He prayed more earnestly: and His sweat was as it were great drops of blood falling down to the ground"*), presenting His genuine human dread of death as a deliberate divine trap that deceived the devil.

[^135]: The Synaxarion for Holy and Great Thursday, composed in Byzantine Greek by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335) and translated into Church Slavonic. It summarizes the four interconnected liturgical mysteries commemorated on this day: (1) the Mandatum or Sacred Washing of the disciples' feet; (2) the Mystical Supper and the institution of the Holy Eucharist; (3) the agony and high-priestly prayer in Gethsemane; and (4) the betrayal of Christ by Judas Iscariot.

[^136]: The Patristic tradition, cited here from Saint Athanasius the Great (ca. 296–373), identifies the anonymous man carrying a pitcher of water (Mark 14:13; Luke 22:10) as Zebedee, the father of the Apostles James and John, who prepared the Upper Room on Mount Sion. Other commentators suggested Joseph of Arimathea or Nicodemus.

[^137]: Saint John Chrysostom, in his *Homilies on the Gospel of Saint John* (Homily 70), explains that Christ intentionally began the washing of feet with Judas, offering the traitor every possible inducement to repentance, before approaching Saint Peter.

[^138]: Chrysostom (*Homilies on the Gospel of Saint Matthew*, Homily 81) observes that Christ revealed the identity of the traitor to the beloved disciple John in a whisper, lest the impetuous and fiery Peter should instantly leap up and kill Judas on the spot to defend his Master.

[^139]: The healing of the high priest's servant Malchus, whose right ear was severed by Peter's sword (Luke 22:50–51; John 18:10–11), is the final recorded miracle of physical healing wrought by Christ before His crucifixion.

[^140]: The Synaxarion addresses the complex harmonization between the Synoptic chronology (which describes the Last Supper as the Passover meal) and the Johannine chronology (John 18:28, indicating that the Jewish authorities postponed eating the Passover until Friday evening to complete Christ's trial and execution). Chrysostom argues that the Jewish leaders deliberately deferred their Passover meal in violation of the Law to carry out their murderous plot against Christ.

[^141]: The Heirmos of Ode 7 invokes the biblical narrative of the Three Holy Children (Ananias, Azarias, and Misael) in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3), whose dew-moistened deliverance prefigures the divine protection of the Church in the midst of worldly persecution.

[^142]: The Heirmos of Ode 8 draws upon the biblical canticle of the Three Children (*Song of the Three Holy Children*, Daniel 3:57–88 LXX), whose steadfast refusal to bow to Nebuchadnezzar's golden image prefigures the Apostles' faithful communion with Christ on Mount Sion.

[^143]: The Heirmos of Ode 9 (*Стра́нствїя Влады́чня* / *Ξενίας δεσποτικῆς*) is one of the most famous and sublime Eucharistic heirmoi in Byzantine hymnography. The Greek term *xenia* denotes both hospitality/welcoming of guests and a festive banquet, celebrating the Upper Room as the celestial chamber where believers participate in the divine life.

[^144]: The second troparion of Ode 9 interprets Proverbs 8:22 (*"The Lord created me as the beginning of His ways..."*), the key proof-text exploited by the Arians, in an orthodox Christological sense: Christ's divine nature was begotten before all ages, whereas His human nature was created in time for the work of our salvation, allowing the incarnate Word to speak in human terms.

[^145]: The third troparion of Ode 9 formulates the orthodox doctrine of the hypostatic union and the communication of idioms (*communicatio idiomatum*): Christ is truly God and truly man, without confusion or separation, working our salvation through both natures united in His single divine Hypostasis.

[^146]: The famous Exapostilarion of Great Week (*Черто́гъ тво́й ви́ждꙋ* / *Тὸν νυμφῶνά σου βλέπω*), based upon the Parable of the Ten Virgins (Matthew 25:1–13) and the Wedding Feast (Matthew 22:11–14), is chanted for the final time at Matins of Holy and Great Thursday.

[^147]: The first sticheron at the Praises (Lauds), Tone 2, vividly depicts the morning gathering of the Sanhedrin, contrasting the judicial pretensions of the mortal judges with the sovereign dignity of the divine Judge of all creation.

[^148]: The Doxastikon of the Praises (*Слава, и нынѣ*), Tone 2, directly cites the third Servant Song of Isaiah (Isaiah 50:6 LXX): *"I gave My back to scourges, and My cheeks to blows; and I turned not away My face from the shame of spittings."*

[^149]: The first Aposticha sticheron, Tone 8, meditates upon the tragic suicide of Judas (Matthew 27:3–5) and the unwitting prophecy of Caiaphas (John 11:49–50: *"It is expedient for us that one man should die for the people, and that the whole nation perish not"*).

[^150]: The second Aposticha sticheron, Tone 8, sharply contrasts Judas's hypocritical concern for the poor at Bethany (John 12:4–6) with his callous commodification of the Savior, selling Him for thirty pieces of silver—the statutory compensation for a dead slave according to Exodus 21:32.

[^151]: The fourth Aposticha sticheron, Tone 8, delivers a solemn liturgical warning to all communicants, urging them to examine their consciences and never approach the Holy Mysteries in the deceitful, treacherous spirit of Judas.

[^152]: The concluding Aposticha theotokion/sticheron, Tone 5, weaves together Christ's farewell discourses from the Gospel of Saint John: the Mandatum of humility (John 13:14–17), the definition of true love and friendship (John 15:12–15), and the allegory of the True Vine (John 15:1–8).

[^153]: The Troparion of the Prophecy at the First Hour, Tone 3, commemorates Christ's patience and forbearance when struck by the officer of the high priest (John 18:22–23).

[^154]: The Old Testament reading at the First Hour: Jeremiah 11:18–12:15 LXX. The passage contains the celebrated prophetic type of Christ as an innocent lamb led to the slaughter (*"I was like a gentle lamb led to the sacrifice"*, Jer. 11:19 LXX), followed by the prophet's perplexity at the prosperity of the wicked and God's sovereign decree of judgment and ultimate restoration.

[^155]: The remarkable phrase in Jeremiah 11:19 LXX—*"Come and let us put wood into his bread"* (*δεῦτε καὶ ἐμβάλωμεν ξύλον εἰς τὸν ἄρτον αὐτοῦ* / *прїиди́те и҆ вложи́мъ дре́во въ хлѣ́бъ є҆гѡ̀*)—is interpreted by the early Church Fathers (Saint Justin Martyr, Tertullian, Saint Cyril of Jerusalem) as a prophetic prefiguration of the Cross (*wood*) placed upon the Eucharistic Body (*bread*) of Christ.

[^156]: Codicological and typikon rubric: From Holy Thursday onward, all memorial litanies (*lity for the reposed* / *панахиди*) in the church narthex and funeral commemorations are suspended throughout Passion Week and Bright Week, resuming only on the Sunday of Saint Thomas (Antipascha), in witness to the joy of Christ's Resurrection which swallows up all death.

[^157]: On Holy and Great Thursday, the Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours are read as simple "three-psalmed" offices without kathisma psalmody or prostrations, reflecting the shift toward the solemn commemoration of the Mystical Supper and Passion.

[^158]: Codicological and iconographic description of the full-width woodcut engraving on fol. 80v (Physical Leaf p88): Christ is depicted at the table of the Mystical Supper, holding the bread in His left hand and raising His right hand in blessing over the Eucharistic chalice. The rubric prescribes the tolling of the semantron at the eighth hour (approximately 2:00 PM) for the solemn celebration of Vespers united with the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great.

[^159]: The rubric prescribes that the priest vest in bright/festive liturgical vestments and perform the Proskomedia (Liturgy of Preparation) during the chanting of the introductory psalmody of Vespers, preparing the Holy Bread for both the Thursday Liturgy and the Reserved Sacrament (*запасні Дари*) consecrated for the sick throughout the coming ecclesiastical year.

[^160]: The solemn Vespers Doxastikon, Tone 6, compares Judas's ungrateful betrayal at the Mystical Supper to the rebellious murmuring of the Israelites who ate the manna in the Sinai wilderness (Psalm 77:18–31 LXX), warning of the eternal perdition that awaits those who treat holy things with contempt.

[^161]: The first Old Testament reading at Thursday Vespers: Exodus 19:10–19 LXX, recounting the terrifying theophany upon Mount Sinai, the sanctification of Israel, and God descending in fire amid thunder and trumpets, serving as the Old Covenant type of the profound sanctification required to approach the New Covenant banquet.

[^162]: The second Old Testament reading at Thursday Vespers: Job 38:1–23 and 42:1–5 LXX, recording God answering Job out of the whirlwind, rebuking mortal presumption and demonstrating the boundless wisdom, power, and transcendence of the Creator, culminating in Job's humble confession of faith.

[^163]: The third Old Testament reading at Thursday Vespers: Isaiah 50:4–11 LXX, the third "Servant Song" of Isaiah, which provides the prophetic script for Christ's voluntary submission to scourging, buffetings, and spittings during His trial.

[^164]: The rubrics at the conclusion of the Old Testament readings mark the seamless transition from Vespers into the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great: the Little Litany is intoned, followed by the chanting of the Trisagion (*Святый Боже*) and the Prokeimenon of the Apostle.

[^165]: The Epistle reading at the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great on Holy and Great Thursday: 1 Corinthians 11:23–32 (zacalo 149). This is the classic Pauline locus of the institution of the Holy Eucharist at the Mystical Supper, delivered by the Apostle as received directly from the Lord ("For I received of the Lord that which also I delivered unto you"). The reading articulates the essential theological and moral requirements for partaking of the Holy Mysteries, warning against receiving the Body and Blood of the Lord unworthily without discerning the Lord's Body, which incurs divine judgment and chastening.

[^166]: The celebrated Troparion of Holy and Great Thursday (*Ве́чери твоеѧ̀ Та́йныѧ дне́сь* / *Τοῦ Δείπνου σου τοῦ μυστικοῦ*), composed in the 5th or 6th century (traditionally attributed to the liturgical tradition of Antioch or Constantinople). On Holy Thursday, it replaces three distinct ordinary chants of the Divine Liturgy: (1) the Cherubic Hymn (*Херувимская пѣснь*) at the Great Entrance, where it is sung thrice in Tone 6; (2) the Communion Verse (*Прича́стенъ* / *Koinonikon*); and (3) the post-communion Thanksgiving Hymn (*"Let our mouths be filled with Thy praise, O Lord"* / *Да исполнятся уста наша*), where it is repeated continuously throughout the communion of the clergy and the faithful. In the wider Byzantine and Ruthenian tradition, it also serves as the universal prayer of personal preparation recited before receiving the Holy Mysteries at every Divine Liturgy.

[^167]: Codicological and Typikon rubrics of the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great on Holy Thursday: At this service, an additional Holy Lamb (*Agnets*) is consecrated, saturated with the Precious Blood, dried according to the prescribed rite in the Sluzhebnik, and reverently placed in the Artophorion (Tabernacle) upon the holy table as the Reserved Sacrament (*запасні Дари*) for the communion of the sick throughout the entire subsequent year. Furthermore, the rubric specifies that the customary post-communion prostrations cease, and that the liturgical Order of the Foot-Washing is celebrated immediately following the Prayer behind the Ambo. At the monastic or parish refectory following the Liturgy, wine and oil are permitted to console the faithful before the severe fast of Great Friday.

[^168]: The Order of the Washing of Feet (*Чинъ Оумове́нїю но́гъ* / *Нιπτήρ* / *Niptēr*) on Holy and Great Thursday. An ancient ceremony attested as early as the 4th-century pilgrimage of Egeria and codified in the Byzantine imperial and monastic typika, commemorating Christ's self-emptying love in washing the feet of the twelve disciples at the Mystical Supper (John 13:1–17). In the Ruthenian Greek Catholic Church, the rite was celebrated with special episcopal solemnity in cathedral churches (such as St. George's Cathedral and the Dormition Church in Lviv), performed by the Metropolitan or Bishop vested in full episcopal vestments, assisted by twelve archpriests, priests, and deacons representing the Apostles.

[^169]: The Heirmos of Ode 5 of the Holy Thursday Matins Canon by Saint Cosmas the Melodist (*Союзомъ любве свѧзуеми апли* / *Συνδέσμῳ ἀγάπης συνδεόμενοι οἱ Ἀπόστολοι*), Tone 6, chanted as the procession of clergy, led by the Hierarch, moves from the sanctuary to the elevated dais prepared in the center of the nave for the Foot-Washing.

[^170]: The Troparia and Stichera Idiomela of the Foot-Washing in Tone 1, meditating on the supreme theological paradox of the Kenosis: the transcendent Creator, Who holds the waters in the clouds and bridles the oceans, kneeling upon the pavement to wash the dust from the feet of mortal clay, thereby conferring a spiritual ladder of ascent over the hostile demonic powers.

[^171]: The solemn Idiomel of the Foot-Washing in Tone 8 (*Дне́сь Непристу́пный существомъ* / *Σήμερον ὁ ἀπρόσιτος τῇ οὐσίᾳ*), presenting the dramatic visual and liturgical contrast between Christ's inaccessible divine essence and His assumption of the form of a bondservant, girding Himself with a linen towel and pouring water into the basin.

[^172]: The dramatic apostrophe to Judas Iscariot in Tone 8 (*Оу́не ти бы́сть Ію́до* / *Κρεῖττόν σοι ἦν Ἰούδα*), directly expanding the tragic prophecy of Christ in Matthew 26:24 and Mark 14:21: *"Woe unto that man by whom the Son of man is betrayed! It had been good for that man if he had not been born."* The hymn contrasts the unbinding of the Apostolic crown with the salvation of the repentant thief on the Cross, and reproaches Judas for hypocritically pretending grief over the precious myrrh of the sinful woman while brazenly selling the innocent Blood of the Righteous One.

[^173]: The Great Litany (*Великая ектенія*) of the Foot-Washing contains special rubrical petitions asking for the sanctification of the water by the descent and operation of the Holy Spirit, that it may serve for the cleansing of the spiritual defilements of the soul. In accordance with the diplomatic and recensional standards of the 1907 Lviv Stauropegion edition, the litany retains the official imperial supplication for Emperor Franz Joseph I of Austria-Hungary (*ѡ бл҃говѣ́рномъ І҆мпера́торѣ на́шемъ*), reflecting the historic liturgical usage of the Galician Metropolia under Austro-Hungarian jurisdiction.

[^174]: The first prayer of the Foot-Washing, read aloud by the Hierarch (*Господи и Боже преблагій*), invokes the Protoevangelium of Genesis 3:15, entreating the Lord to preserve the souls and bodies of the faithful from the deceitful serpent that lies in wait for the human heel, and granting spiritual authority to tread upon serpents and scorpions and all the power of the enemy (alluding to Psalm 90:13 LXX and Luke 10:19).

[^175]: The second prayer of the Foot-Washing, recited secretly by the Hierarch (*втай глаголемая*) while all present bow their heads. It meditates upon Christ's pedagogical inversion of worldly power (Luke 22:24–27), wherein He designated the last to be first, teaching His disciples that true greatness in the Kingdom of God consists in sacrificial service to one's brethren.

[^176]: The First Gospel Pericope of the Foot-Washing: John 13:3–11 (section 44). The liturgical text is structured as a living liturgical drama: as the protodeacon reads the sacred narrative, the Hierarch performs the physical actions simultaneously in real time—divesting himself of his episcopal vestments (omophorion, sakkos, klobuk), girding his loins with a linen towel (*lention* / *obrus*), pouring warm water into the copper basin, and kneeling on both knees before each of the twelve priests in succession, beginning on the left side, to wash their feet, dry them with the towel, and kiss them reverently.

[^177]: The dramatic dialogue between Christ and Saint Peter: When the Hierarch reaches the senior archpriest seated in the place of Saint Peter (*обдержай мѣсто Петрово*), the deacon's continuous reading pauses and the two celebrants chant the biblical exchange verbatim. When Christ insists that without this washing Peter has no part with Him, the archpriest representing Peter rises in fear and astonishment, gestures with his hands toward his feet, then elevates his hands, and finally bows his head, crying aloud: *"Lord, not my feet only, but also my hands and my head!"*

[^178]: The Second Gospel Pericope of the Foot-Washing: John 13:12–17 (section 45), chanted by the Hierarch himself from the ambo or episcopal cathedra after he has finished the foot-washing, resumed his episcopal vestments, and sat down again, delivering Christ's solemn dominical commandment of mutual brotherly service: *"For I have given you an example, that ye should do as I have done to you."*

[^179]: The concluding prayer of the Foot-Washing (*Господи Боже нашъ, по мнозѣй милости твоей излаявый себе*), followed by the sprinkling of the clergy and faithful with the blessed water from the basin. The troparion in Tone 6 (*Оумывше нозѣ* / *Νιψάμενοι τοὺς πόδας*) commemorates the Apostles departing from the Upper Room on Mount Sion to accompany the Lord across the brook Cedron to the Mount of Olives.

[^180]: Small Compline on Holy and Great Thursday, featuring the dramatic Tri-ode (*трипѣ́снецъ*) composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), containing Odes 5, 8, and 9 in Tone 8. Unlike the theological and typological canon of St. Cosmas sung at Matins, St. Andrew's hymnody is profoundly psychological and dramatic, vividly portraying the agony of Gethsemane, the deceit of Judas's honeyed kiss, the weeping of Peter, the cowardly hand-washing of Pontius Pilate, and the blood-thirsty demands of the Sanhedrin.

[^181]: Iconographic and codicological description of the full-width woodcut engraving on fol. 91r (Physical Leaf p99): The Mocking and Crowning with Thorns. Christ is depicted bound and seated, clad in a mock-regal robe and holding a reed as a mock scepter, while Roman soldiers press the crown of thorns upon His head with staves and mockingly bend the knee before Him. The engraving is signed at the bottom by the 19th-century Galician wood-engraver A. Jędrzejowski / Jewecki.

[^182]: The monumental Office of the Holy and Saving Passions of Our Lord Jesus Christ (*Послѣ́дованїе ст҃ы́хъ и҆ сп҃си́тельныхъ Страсте́й Гдⷭ҇а на́шегѡ І҆и҃са Хрⷭ҇та̀*), celebrated on Thursday evening as the Matins of Holy and Great Friday. Commonly known as the "Service of the Twelve Passion Gospels" (*Стра́сти*), this office constitutes the liturgical summit of Holy Week. Its structure consists of twelve continuous Gospel pericopes (the *Passion Gospels*) extracted from all four Evangelists, harmonizing the entire historical account of Christ's Passion from the Farewell Discourse in the Upper Room to His death on Golgotha and burial by Joseph of Arimathea.

[^183]: The celebrated Troparion of Great Friday Matins (*Егда́ сла́внїи ᲂу҆чени́цы* / *Ὅτε οἱ ἔνδοξοι μαθηταί*), Tone 8, chanted three times slowly and melodiously (*ко́сно и со сладкопѣ́нїемъ*), during which the ecclesiarch distributes lighted beeswax candles to all the clergy and worshippers in the darkened temple, symbolizing the divine light of Christ shining into the darkness of the Passion and the betrayal of Judas.

[^184]: The First Passion Gospel: John 13:31–18:1 (section 46). This is the longest of the Twelve Gospels, containing Christ's solemn Farewell Discourse and High-Priestly Prayer in the Upper Room, comforting His disciples, promising the descent of the Holy Spirit the Comforter, establishing the new commandment of love, and concluding as Christ leads the Eleven out across the brook Cedron into the Garden of Gethsemane.

[^185]: The opening troparia of Antiphon 1 in Tone 8 (*Кня́зи лю́дстїи собра́шасѧ вкꙋ́пѣ* / *Οἱ ἄρχοντες τῶν λαῶν συνήχθησαν*), based directly upon the messianic prophecy of Psalm 2:2 LXX: *"The kings of the earth stood up, and the rulers gathered themselves together, against the Lord, and against His Christ."* This initiates the cycle of fifteen Antiphons of Great Friday Matins, which alternate between the first eight Passion Gospels.

[^186]: Sessional Hymn (Kathisma) in Tone 7 chanted after Antiphon 3 and the First Passion Gospel. The accompanying rubric specifically directs: *(Не сѣди́мъ же на сицевь́хъ сѣда́льнахъ, за є҆́же сщ҃е́нникꙋ кади́ти ст҃ы́й ѻ҆лта́рь, но стоя́ще пое́мъ ѧ҆̀)* — "We do not sit at such sessional hymns, because the priest censes the holy altar, but standing we sing them." In the liturgical structure of the Passion Matins, sessional hymns follow every three antiphons (after the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th, and 5th groups of antiphons), accompanied by a censing of the holy table and the entire nave, while the faithful remain standing holding lighted candles. The hymn meditates on the Mystical Supper, where Christ nourished His disciples while convicting Judas of his covert treachery.

[^187]: The Second Passion Gospel: John 18:1–28 (section 58). This pericope covers the departure of Jesus and His disciples across the brook Cedron into the garden of Gethsemane; the arrival of Judas with the cohort of soldiers and temple officers bearing lanterns, torches, and weapons; Christ's majestic self-revelation ("I am He") causing them to draw back and fall to the ground; Peter striking the high priest's servant Malchus; Christ's arrest and binding; His being led first to Annas; Peter's first denial outside the high priest's court; the interrogation of Jesus concerning His disciples and doctrine; the officer striking Jesus with the palm of his hand; and Christ being sent bound unto Caiaphas the high priest.

[^188]: Sessional Hymn in Tone 7 chanted after Antiphon 6 and the Second Passion Gospel (*Кі́й тѧ̀ ѻ҆́бразъ І҆ꙋ́до, преда́телѧ Сп҃сꙋ содѣ́ла* / *Ποῖός σε τρόπος Ἰούδα, προδότην τοῦ Σωτῆρος εἰργάσατο*). A celebrated patristic and hymnographic interrogation of Judas's motives, contrasting the singular privileges granted to him as an Apostle—sharing in the Apostolic choir, the charismatic gift of performing healings, reclining at the Mystical Supper, and having his feet washed by the Creator—with his base ingratitude and avarice. The hymn concludes by magnifying Christ's boundless long-suffering and great mercy.

[^189]: The Third Passion Gospel: Matthew 26:57–75 (section 109). Christ is brought before Caiaphas the high priest, where the scribes and elders are assembled; false witnesses seek testimony against Him; Caiaphas adjures Him by the living God to declare whether He is the Christ, the Son of God; Jesus confesses His divine identity and session at the right hand of Power; the high priest rends his clothes and condemns Him for blasphemy; the council spits in His face, buffets Him, and strikes Him with the palms of their hands; simultaneously, Peter sits in the courtyard, where he is recognized three times and denies the Lord with an oath; the cock crows, Peter remembers the word of the Lord, and he goes out weeping bitterly.

[^190]: Sessional Hymn in Tone 8 chanted after Antiphon 9 and the Third Passion Gospel (*Ѽ ка́кѡ І҆ꙋ́да и҆ногда̀ тво́й ᲂу҆чн҃къ* / *Ὦ πῶς Ἰούδας ποτὲ σοῦ ὁ μαθητής*). The hymn contrasts Judas's former fellowship as an Apostle with his treacherous bargain with the chief priests, whom he approached with hypocritical zeal, claiming to deliver Him Who "hath broken the Law and defiled the Sabbath."

[^191]: The Fourth Passion Gospel: John 18:28–19:16 (section 54). Christ is led early from Caiaphas unto the Roman praetorium before Pontius Pilate; the Jews refuse to enter the praetorium lest they be defiled; Pilate interrogates Jesus within concerning His kingship; Christ delivers His classic declaration that His Kingdom is not of this world, and that He came into the world to bear witness unto the truth; Pilate utters his cynical question "What is truth?" and attempts to release Jesus according to the Passover custom; the crowd demands Barabbas the robber; Pilate scourges Jesus and the soldiers crown Him with thorns, clothe Him in a purple robe, and strike Him; Pilate presents Him to the multitude with the immortal words "Behold the Man!" (*Ἴδε ὁ ἄνθρωπος* / *Сѐ Человѣ́къ*); the chief priests cry "Crucify Him!"; Pilate washes his hands of His blood; and upon the chief priests' declaration "We have no king but Caesar," Pilate delivers Him over to be crucified.

[^192]: Greek recensional variant troparion in Antiphon 10 (*Оу҆мирѝ мі́ръ, ѿ Дв҃ы и҆зво́ливый Гдⷭ҇и* / *Εἰρήνευσον τὸν κόσμον, ὁ ἐκ Παρθένου ἑλόμενος Κύριε*). In the 1907 Lviv Stauropegion edition, this troparion is printed with the explicit editorial rubrical heading `[ѿ Гре́ческагѡ.]` ("From the Greek") enclosed in brackets. This witnesses to the historical scholarly and recensional collation performed by the Stauropegion editors, incorporating Greek Triodion hymnographic witnesses into the Church Slavonic textus receptus.

[^193]: Sessional Hymn in Tone 8 chanted after Antiphon 12 and the Fourth Passion Gospel (*Е҆гда̀ предста́лъ є҆сѝ Каїа́фѣ Бж҃е* / *Ἡνίκα τῷ Καϊάφᾳ παρίστασο ὁ Θεός*). The hymn depicts the cosmic dread of the heavenly powers at the sight of the Supreme Judge standing before earthly tribunals (Caiaphas and Pilate), and His willing ascent upon the Cross between two thieves, numbered among the transgressors (Isaiah 53:12) to save fallen mankind.

[^194]: The Fifth Passion Gospel: Matthew 27:3–32 (section 111). Judas repents, confesses that he has betrayed innocent blood, casts down the thirty pieces of silver in the temple, and goes and hangs himself; the chief priests purchase the potter's field with the blood-money, fulfilling the prophecy of Zechariah/Jeremiah (Matthew 27:9–10); Jesus stands before Pilate the governor and remains silent; Pilate's wife sends word of her dream warning him to have nothing to do with that just man; the multitude chooses Barabbas and cries "Let Him be crucified!"; the Roman soldiers mock Christ in the praetorium, stripping Him, arraying Him in a scarlet robe, plaiting a crown of thorns upon His head, and placing a reed in His right hand; and leading Him away to Golgotha, they compel Simon of Cyrene to bear His cross.

[^195]: The Fifteenth Antiphon of Great Friday Matins, Tone 6 (*Дне́сь ви́ситъ на дре́вѣ* / *Σήμερον κρεμᾶται ἐπὶ ξύλου*). This hymn is universally recognized as the poetic and theological masterpiece of Great Friday hymnography. Composed in a sequence of six majestic parallel antitheses contrasting Christ's divine sovereignty with His kenotic humiliation: (1) He Who suspended the earth upon the waters hangs upon the Tree; (2) the King of angels is crowned with thorns; (3) He Who wraps the heavens in clouds is wrapped in a mock purple robe; (4) He Who freed Adam in the Jordan receives blows upon the face; (5) the Bridegroom of the Church is transfixed with nails; and (6) the Son of the Virgin is pierced with a lance. The antiphon concludes with the celebrated triple prostration: "We worship Thy Sufferings, O Christ! / We worship Thy Sufferings, O Christ! / We worship Thy Sufferings, O Christ! / Show us also Thy glorious Resurrection!"

[^196]: The Sessional Hymn (Kathisma / Troparion) in Tone 4 chanted after the 15th Antiphon and prior to the Sixth Passion Gospel (*И҆скꙋпи́лъ ны̀ є҆сѝ ѿ кла́твы зако́нныѧ* / *Ἐξηγόρασας ἡμᾶς ἐκ τῆς κατάρας τοῦ νόμου*). Based directly upon Galatians 3:13 ("Christ hath redeemed us from the curse of the law, being made a curse for us: for it is written, Cursed is every one that hangs on a tree"), this troparion summarizes the soteriological doctrine of the Atonement: by Christ's voluntary Crucifixion, piercing with the lance, and shedding of His precious Blood upon Golgotha, mankind is delivered from the condemnation of the Mosaic Law and endowed with divine immortality.

[^197]: The Sixth Passion Gospel: Mark 15:16–32 (section 67). This pericope recounts the mocking of Christ by the Roman cohort in the praetorium, where they clothe Him in purple, crown Him with thorns, strike Him upon the head with a reed, spit upon Him, and bow their knees before Him in derision; His procession to Golgotha, with Simon of Cyrene compelled to bear His cross; His refusal of the wine mingled with myrrh; His crucifixion at the third hour; the division of His garments by casting lots; the superscription of His accusation ("The King of the Jews"); His crucifixion between two thieves; the mockery of the passers-by, chief priests, and scribes ("He saved others; Himself He cannot save... Let Christ the King of Israel descend now from the cross, that we may see and believe"); and the reviling of the thieves crucified with Him.

[^198]: Psalm 21:19 (LXX 21:18): *Раздѣли́ша ри̑зы моѧ̑ себѣ̀, и҆ ѡ҆ ѻ҆де́жди мое́й мета́ша жре́бїй* ("They parted My garments among them, and upon My vesture they cast lots"). In the liturgical structure of Passion Matins, this prokeimenon in Tone 4 introduces the Seventh Passion Gospel, highlighting the direct fulfillment of Davidic messianic prophecy in the crucifixion narrative.

[^199]: The Seventh Passion Gospel: Matthew 27:33–54 (section 113). The arrival at Golgotha; offering of vinegar mingled with gall; the crucifixion and casting lots for His garments; the setting up of the accusation ("This is Jesus the King of the Jews"); the railing of the passers-by and elders; the darkness over all the land from the sixth unto the ninth hour; Christ's loud cry at the ninth hour: *"Eli, Eli, lama sabachthani"*; the offering of the sponge filled with vinegar on a reed; Christ crying again with a loud voice and yielding up the ghost; the tearing in twain of the temple veil from top to bottom; the earthquake and rending of rocks; the opening of the graves and resurrection of many bodies of the saints; and the confession of the centurion and those watching Jesus: *"Truly this was the Son of God."*

[^200]: Rubric for patristic reading: *(И҆ полага́етсѧ чте́нїе)* — "And a reading is appointed." Following the Seventh Passion Gospel and preceding Psalm 50, traditional liturgical typika (including the Evergetis, Studite, and Ruthenian Stauropegion traditions) appoint a solemn patristic homily on the Lord's Passion, typically selected from St. John Chrysostom, St. Ephrem the Syrian, or St. Cyril of Alexandria.

[^201]: The Eighth Passion Gospel: Luke 23:32–49 (section 114). The leading away of two evildoers to be put to death with Jesus at Calvary; Christ's prayer for His executioners: *"Father, forgive them; for they know not what they do"*; the dividing of His garments; the derision of the rulers and soldiers offering vinegar; the superscription in letters of Greek, Latin, and Hebrew; the dialogue of the two thieves, wherein the repentant thief confesses Christ's innocence and cries: *"Lord, remember me when Thou comest into Thy kingdom"*, receiving the Lord's divine promise: *"Truly I say unto thee, Today shalt thou be with Me in paradise"*; the darkness over all the earth until the ninth hour; the sun darkened and veil rent; Christ's final commendation: *"Father, into Thy hands I commend My spirit"*; and the centurion glorifying God and declaring: *"Certainly this was a righteous man."*

[^202]: The Three-Ode Canon (Triodion / Tripesnets) of Great Friday Matins, composed by St. Cosmas the Melodist (Bishop of Maiuma, c. 675–752). Comprising Odes 5, 8, and 9 in Tone 6, the canon directly reflects Byzantine hymnographic structure for the Holy and Great Friday vigil. The rubric directs the heirmoi to be chanted twice and the troparia twelve times (interpolating the stichological refrains between the troparia), concluding with a joint katavasia by both choirs chanting the heirmos together.

[^203]: The acrostic (*краегране́сїе*) of St. Cosmas's Three-Ode Canon: *Проса́вватонъ те* (Greek: Προσάββατόν τε), signifying "And the eve of the Sabbath" (the day before the Sabbath, i.e., Friday). Across Odes 5, 8, and 9, the twelve troparia spell out this exact Greek acrostic in their original Byzantine verses, preserved here faithfully in Church Slavonic translation with the acrostic letters explicitly indicated in the margins: Ode 5 (`П`, `Р`, `О`), Ode 8 (`С`, `А`, `В`, `В`, `А`), and Ode 9 (`Т`, `О`, `Н`, `Т`, `Є`).

[^204]: The Kontakion of Holy and Great Friday in Tone 8 (*На́съ ра́ди Распѧ́таго, прїиди́те всѝ воспои́мъ* / *Τὸν δι' ἡμᾶς σταυρωθέντα, δεῦτε πάντες ὑμνήσωμεν*), composed by St. Romanos the Melodist (6th century). This kontakion introduces Romanos's celebrated poetic dialogue between the Virgin Mary and her Son on His way to Golgotha, emphasizing that Mary's confession of faith transcends the shame of the Cross: *"Though Thou dost endure crucifixion, Thou art my Son and my God!"*

[^205]: The Ikos of Holy and Great Friday (*Своего̀ А҆́гнца А҆́гница зрѧ́щи* / *Τὸν ἴδιον Ἄρνα θεωροῦσα ἡ Ἀμνάς*), forming the strophe of St. Romanos's kontakion. The Virgin Mary, seeing her Lamb led to slaughter, addresses Him with maternal pathos, asking whether He is hastening to another wedding in Cana of Galilee to turn water into wine, and begging the divine Logos not to pass her by in silence, but to speak a word of comfort to the Mother who bare Him.

[^206]: The Synaxarion of Holy and Great Friday, compiled by the 14th-century Byzantine ecclesiastical historian Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos. The opening distich verses on the Crucifixion (*Живы́й є҆сѝ Бж҃е ты̀, и҆ ᲂу҆мерщвле́нный на дре́вѣ* / *Ζωὸς πέφυκας, ὦ Θεός, νεκρὸς δὲ σύ· Ὦ νεκρὲ γυμνέ, καὶ Θεοῦ ζῶντος Λόγε*) and on the thief (*Заключе́ннаѧ ѿве́рзе Е҆де́мскаѧ врата̀* / *Κεκλεισμένας ἤνοιξε τῆς Ἐδὲμ πύλας, Βαλὼν ὁ λῃστὴς κλεῖδα, τὸ Μνήσθητί μου*) encapsulate the cosmic paradox of the Creator dying in the flesh and opening paradise through the repentant thief's prayer. The narrative prose recounts the full chronological progression of Christ's trials before Annas, Caiaphas, and Pilate, the release of Barabbas, the scourging and crowning with thorns, the crucifixion at Golgotha, the supernatural portents, the piercing of the side, and the burial by Joseph and Nicodemus.

[^207]: *Lithostrotos* (Greek: Λιθόστρωτον; Hebrew/Aramaic: Gabbatha, John 19:13): the elevated stone pavement where Pilate sat on the judgment seat. The Synaxarion connects this biblical site with the Jewish and early Christian theological tradition regarding Solomon covering Adam's skull with a mound of stones, whence the name of the place was derived.

[^208]: The patristic theological exposition of the Crucifixion in the Synaxarion: St. Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos expounds the typology of the Passion: Christ is crucified on Friday (the 6th day) and at the 6th hour because man was created on the 6th day and Adam stretched forth his hand to the tree at the 6th hour; the garden of the tomb corresponds to the Garden of Eden; the bitter drink heals the tasting of the forbidden fruit; the buffeting confers freedom; the spittings restore human honor; the crown of thorns removes the curse of the earth; the scarlet robe replaces the coats of skins; the nails arrest the movement of sin; the pierced side heals the wound of Adam's rib from which Eve was formed; the lance wards off the cherubim's flaming sword; the water from the side prefigures baptism; and the blood poured forth upon Adam's skull beneath Golgotha signifies the mystical baptism and redemption of fallen humanity by the New Adam.

[^209]: The Exapostilarion of Holy and Great Friday (*Разбо́йника бл҃горазꙋ́мнаго во є҆ди́номъ часѣ̀ ра́еви сподо́билъ є҆сѝ Гдⷭ҇и* / *Τὸν λῃστὴν αὐθημερὸν τοῦ παραδείσου ἠξίωσας, Κύριε*). Chanted thrice with great solemnity at the conclusion of the Three-Ode Canon, this hymn is one of the most beloved masterpieces of the Passion services, begging the Savior to enlighten the believer by the wood of the Cross even as He received the repentant thief into paradise.

[^210]: The Ninth Passion Gospel: John 19:25–37 (section 61). Standing by the Cross: His Mother, Mary the wife of Cleopas, and Mary Magdalene; Christ commending His Mother unto the beloved disciple John: *"Woman, behold thy son!"* and to the disciple: *"Behold thy mother!"*; Christ's cry: *"I thirst"*; the sponge filled with vinegar put upon hyssop and placed to His mouth; Christ's triumph: *"It is finished"*; bowing His head and giving up the ghost; the soldiers breaking the legs of the first and the other thief, but finding Jesus already dead, they broke not His legs, fulfilling Scripture (*"A bone of Him shall not be broken"*, Ex. 12:46, Ps. 33:21); one of the soldiers piercing His side with a lance, and forthwith blood and water coming out; and the testimony of the eyewitness disciple, fulfilling Zechariah 12:10 (*"They shall look on Him Whom they pierced"*).

[^211]: The Doxastikon (Glory) of the Praises (Lauds) in Tone 6 (*Совлеко́ша съ менѐ ри̑зы моѧ̑* / *Ἐξέδυσάν με τὰ ἱμάτιά μου*).
