Matins features Tone 5 resurrectional stichera highlighting the confusion of the guards and the mystery of the sealed tomb. At the Liturgy, the Epistle (Acts 2:38-43, pericope 7) records the baptism of three thousand souls on Pentecost, and the Gospel (John 3:1-15, pericope 8) presents Christ's nocturnal discourse with Nicodemus regarding regeneration of water and the Spirit.

[^438]: Bright Thursday Vespers (for Bright Friday). Advancing to Tone 6 of the Octoechos, the service features the solemn chanting of the Great Prokeimenon in Tone 7: "I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength: the Lord is my foundation and my refuge!" (Ps. 17:2-3a [18:1-2a]), accompanied by three responsory verses from Psalm 17 [18], celebrating God's triumphant deliverance of His servant from death and the nethermost pit.

[^439]: Bright Friday Matins and Divine Liturgy (Въ Пѧто́къ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы). In Byzantine and Slavic tradition, Bright Friday is also widely observed with the commemoration of the Life-Giving Spring (*Живоно́сный Исто́чникъ*) of the Mother of God. The Pentecostarion text appoints the Tone 6 resurrectional stichera at Praises, followed at Liturgy by the Epistle (Acts 3:1-8, pericope 7), describing the miraculous healing of the lame man at the Beautiful Gate of the Temple, and the Gospel (John 2:12-22, pericope 7), recounting the cleansing of the Temple in Jerusalem and Christ's prophecy concerning the temple of His Body.

[^440]: Bright Friday Vespers (for Bright Saturday). The liturgical cycle reaches the final mode of the week, Tone 8 of the Octoechos. The stichera at "Lord, I have cried" celebrate the harrowing of hades, concluding with the Dogmatic Theotokion of Tone 8: "The King of heaven out of love for mankind appeared on earth..." (Цр҃ь нб҃ный за чл҃вѣколю́бїе на землѝ ꙗ҆ви́сѧ), which articulates the Chalcedonian confession of Christ's two natures in one hypostasis without confusion or separation.

[^441]: Bright Saturday Matins (Въ Сꙋббѡ́тꙋ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы на Оу҆́трени). The concluding morning service of Bright Week, bringing the week-long succession through the Octoechos tones to completion in Tone 8. At the conclusion of the Divine Liturgy on this day, the ancient prayer for the breaking and distribution of the Artos (*раздроблѐнїе А҆́ртоса*) is recited, and the Royal Doors of the iconostasis are closed prior to the celebration of the Ninth Hour and Thomas Sunday Vespers.

[^442]: Bright Saturday Epistle and Gospel: Acts 3:11-16 (pericope 8), proclaiming Saint Peter's address in Solomon's Porch after the healing of the lame man, testifying that the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob hath glorified His Servant Jesus, Whom the Jews delivered up, but God raised from the dead; and John 3:22-33 (pericope 11), recounting Saint John the Baptist's final testimony unto Christ in Judea ("He that hath the bride is the bridegroom... He must increase, but I must decrease"). In addition, this leaf codifies the monastic typikon for the solemn elevation, distribution, and consumption of the Artos (*раздроблѐнїе А҆́ртоса*) and the elevation of the Panagia bread at the refectory trapeza concluding Bright Week.

[^443]: Antipascha or Sunday of Saint Thomas (*Недѣ́лѧ А҆нтїпа́схи, є҆́же є҆́сть ѡ҆пѧза́нїе ст҃а́гѡ сла́внагѡ А҆пⷭ҇ла Ѳѡмы̀* / Ἀντίπασχα), the Octave of Pascha, also designated Renewal Sunday (*Но́ваѧ Недѣ́лѧ* / Dominica in albis), commemorating the second appearance of the risen Christ unto the Eleven gathered behind closed doors eight days after His Resurrection, and the touching of His side and nail-prints by Saint Thomas (John 20:19-31).

[^444]: The Festal Troparion (Troparion) of Thomas Sunday in Tone 7: "Though the tomb was sealed, Thou didst shine forth Life from the tomb, O Christ God..." (Запеча́танꙋ гро́бꙋ, живо́тъ ѿ гро́ба возсїѧ́лъ є҆сѝ). Chanted at Small and Great Vespers, Matins, and Divine Liturgy throughout the post-festive week until the Apodosis of the Feast, this hymn connects Christ's emergence from the sealed sepulchre with His passage through closed doors, bestowing the Holy Spirit upon the disciples for the renewal of humanity according to His abundant mercy.

[^445]: The Festal Idiomela at "Lord, I have cried" for the Sunday of Renewal (*Но́ваѧ Недѣ́лѧ*), authored by Saint John of Damascus ("John the Monk"). The hymnody focuses upon the physiological and empirical reality of the resurrected Body of Christ: entering through locked doors without breaching their physical integrity, yet offering tangible flesh, bones, and wounds to be touched, thereby proving that He was neither a disembodied phantom nor an ordinary mortal.

[^446]: The Festal Litiya hymn in Tone 4, singing of Christ's twofold nature (*твоѐ сꙋгꙋ́бое сꙋщество̀*): "O Lord, by the unendurable radiance of Thy Divinity..." Thomas's tactile examination of the Savior's pierced Side, hands, and feet serves as a dogmatic witness to the unconfused union of the divine and human natures in the one Hypostasis of the Incarnate Word.

[^447]: The Aposticha idiomelon in Tone 4 comparing Saint John the Evangelist and Saint Thomas: "O most marvelous wonder! Grass, touching fire, was saved... John reclined upon the breast of the Word, and Thomas was counted worthy to touch His Side..." While John drew forth the sublime theology of the pre-eternal Word at the Mystical Supper, Thomas obtained the physical proof of the bodily Resurrection, providing an irrefutable apologetic witness for subsequent generations.

[^448]: The Polyeleos Megalynarion (Magnification) of Thomas Sunday: "We magnify Thee, O Christ Life-giver, Who for our sake didst descend into hades, and hast raised all with Thyself" (Велича́емъ тѧ̀ Живода́вче Хрⷭ҇тѐ). The eight appointed psalm verses are drawn from Psalm 92:1a, Psalm 106:16, Psalm 106:14, Psalm 77:65, Psalm 67:2 [68:1], Psalm 117:24 [118:24], Psalm 9:33 [10:12], and Psalm 29:13 [30:12].

[^449]: The Canon of Thomas Sunday in Tone 1, composed by Saint John of Damascus ("John the Monk"), chanted with the heirmoi of the Paschal Canon ("The Day of Resurrection! Let us be radiant, O ye peoples...") as Katavasia. Following Ode 3 and the Hypakoe in Tone 6, the rubrics appoint the Patristic reading from Saint Gregory the Theologian: Oration 44, *In novam Dominicam* (PG 36:608-621; incipit: *Ѡ҆бновле́нїемъ чти́тисѧ дрѐвнїй зако́нъ* / "To honor renewals is an ancient law").

[^450]: The Kontakion and Ikos of Thomas Sunday in Tone 8, composed by Saint Romanos the Melodist. The Ikos employs vivid theological imagery (*Кто̀ сохранѝ ᲂу҆чени́чꙋ дла́нь тогда̀ неѡпали́мꙋ...*): "Who preserved the disciple's hand unburnt at that time, when he approached the fiery Side of the Lord?", likening Thomas's hand to Moses' burning bush (Exodus 3:2) and the Three Holy Youths in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3), protected by divine power while touching the incandescent Godhead.

[^451]: The Synaxarion of the Sunday of Saint Thomas from the Byzantine Menologion, attributed to Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335). It expounds the theological significance of Renewal Sunday as the eighth day (*ѻ҆сми́ца*) prefiguring the unending, nightless Day of eternity in the age to come, explains the divine pedagogy in allowing Thomas to doubt for eight days, and explores the etymology of Didymus ("Twin", as doubting, born a twin, or possessing conjoined fingers).

[^452]: *Tone 1, Podoben: "Thou Who didst adorn heaven with stars" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ ѕвѣзда́ми)* is the classical festive Exaposteilarion model melody (*Ὁ οὐρανὸν τοῖς ἄστροις κατακοσμήσας*), here abbreviated by its Slavonic incipit *Ꙗ҆́кѡ ѕвѣзда́ми* rather than the full *Не́бо ѕвѣзда́ми оу҆краси́вый*.

[^453]: The monastic catecheses (*oglasheniya*) of Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826), abbot of the Studion Monastery in Constantinople, traditionally read in the narthex during the Paschaltide processions (*litiya*) before the First Hour.

[^454]: The liturgical readings for Sunday of Saint Thomas Liturgy: the Apostle reading from the Acts of the Apostles, pericope 14 (Acts 5:12–20), and the Holy Gospel according to John, pericope 65 (John 20:19–31), recording Christ's appearance on the eighth day and the confession of Thomas.

[^455]: The Great Prokeimenon *Who is so great a god as our God?* (Ps. 76:14–15, 11, 12) is chanted in Tone 7 at festive Vespers on Pascha Sunday evening and on the evening of the Sunday of Antipascha (Saint Thomas Sunday).

[^456]: Saint Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), prolific Byzantine melodist and hymnographer, author of the weekday triodia and tetraodia included in the Pentecostarion across the Paschal season.

[^457]: The monastic rubric notes that the Paschaltide Midnight Office (*Polunochnitsa*) was commonly recited individually in cells (*v keliyakh*) during the festive octave rather than chanted in common choir in the nave.

[^458]: The Patristic homily assigned at Matins of Thomas Week: readings from Saint John Chrysostom's *Commentary on the Gospel of Saint John* (Homilies 86 and 87, expounding John 20:19–31).

[^459]: The Inter-Hours (*Mesoria*, Church Slavonic *Mezhdochasie*) are the four supplementary mid-hour offices inserted between the First, Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours, resumed on the Monday of Saint Thomas Week following the Bright Week suspension.

[^460]: The liturgical readings for Monday of Thomas Week Liturgy: Acts pericope 8 (Acts 3:19–26) and John pericope 6 (John 2:1–11, the Marriage at Cana).

[^461]: Rubrical and recensional note documenting the liturgical variance between Ruthenian/Slavonic editions (which print the weekday Triodia of Saint Joseph in the Pentecostarion for Liturgy/Matins) and Byzantine Greek usage (which assigns them to Compline together with the Theotokion Canon).

[^462]: The Greek alphabetical acrostic *ᾄδει δὲ σὲ Ἰωσήф* ("Joseph also hymns Thee"), preserved in the printed Slavonic edition with individual marginal letters (*А, Д, Є, І, Д, Є, С, Є, І, Ѡ, С, И, Ф*) marking the successive troparia across Odes 1, 8, and 9 of the Triodion.

[^463]: *Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ)* is the widely used Prosomoion melody *Ὡς γενναῖον ἐν μάρτυσιν* (*Я́кѡ до́бля въ мꙋ́ченицѣхъ*), originating from the feast of Saint George the Great-Martyr.

[^464]: The festal troparion of Antipascha (Saint Thomas Sunday) in Tone 7: *Запеча́танꙋ гро́бꙋ, живо́тъ и҆з̾ гро́ба возсїя́лъ є҆сѝ Хрⷭ҇тѐ Бж҃е...* ("When the stone had been sealed..."), printed on fol. 250 (physical leaf p255).

[^465]: Allusion to Christ's Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard (Matt. 20:1–16), wherein those hired at the eleventh hour receive equal wages through the unmerited grace and bounty of the Householder.

[^466]: St. John the Monk (Saint John of Damascus, c. 676–749), author of the idiomelon *Две́ремъ заключе́ннымъ, прише́лъ є҆сѝ Хрⷭ҇тѐ...*, celebrated for his profound dogmatic precision in synthesizing the bodily reality of Christ's post-Resurrection appearances.

[^467]: The Greek acrostic *Ψαλῶ Θεῷ Ἰωσήφ* ("I Joseph will sing unto God"), preserved in the printed Slavonic edition through marginal Greek/Slavonic letters marking the strophes of Odes 2, 8, and 9 of the Triodion composed by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886).

[^468]: *Tone 1, Podoben: "O all-lauded martyrs" (Прехва́льнїи мꙋ́ченицы)* is the classical Prosomoion melody *Πανεύφημοι μάρτυρες*, originally composed for the common commemoration of martyrs and widely used across the Octoechos and Pentecostarion.

[^469]: A precise dogmatic assertion of Dyothelitism and Dyoenergism affirmed by the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681): Christ possesses two natural operations (*дѣ́йства два̀ сꙋ́щественна*, *δύο φυσικὰς ἐνεργείας*) corresponding to His two intact natures, divine and human, without division or confusion.

[^470]: Allusion to Christ's words in Luke 24:39: "Behold My hands and My feet, that it is I Myself: handle Me, and see; for a spirit hath not flesh and bones, as ye see Me have."

[^471]: The festal troparion of Antipascha (Saint Thomas Sunday) in Tone 7: *Запеча́танꙋ гро́бꙋ...* ("When the stone had been sealed..."), printed on fol. 250 (physical leaf p255).

[^472]: The liturgical order of the Octoechos and Pentecostarion gives precedence to the Cross over the Resurrection on Wednesdays and Fridays (the fast days commemorating Christ's betrayal and crucifixion), reflecting the historical sequence of the saving Passion preceding the third-day Rising.

[^473]: Patristic typological pairing: Isaac led up Mount Moriah (Gen. 22) prefiguring Christ bound and led to Golgotha as the sacrificial Lamb, and Jonah three days in the sea-monster (Jonah 1:17; Matt. 12:40) prefiguring Christ's three-day burial and bodily resurrection.

[^474]: *Tone 1, Podoben: "While the soldiers were guarding Thy tomb" (Гро́бъ тво́й Сп҃се)* is the venerable Resurrectional Sessional melody *Τὸν τάφον σου Σωτήρ*, sung throughout the Paschal season.

[^475]: The Greek acrostic *Αἰνῶ Θεόν, Ἰωσήφ* ("I praise God, Joseph"), marking the successive strophes of Odes 3, 8, and 9 of the Wednesday Triodion composed by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer.

[^476]: *Tone 2, Podoben: "When from the Tree" (Є҆гда̀ ѿ дре́ва)* is the classic Prosomoion melody *Ὅτε ἐκ τοῦ ξύλου*, originally sung on Great and Holy Friday for the Deposition from the Cross.

[^477]: A rhythmic liturgical panegyric stringing together the festal attributes of Pascha (*Па́сха та́йнаѧ, Па́сха Бж҃е́ственнаѧ, Па́сха спаси́тельнаѧ...*), directly evoking the lyrical style of the Paschal Stichera.

[^478]: Theological exposition of John 20:25–28: Thomas's tactile examination of the sacred wounds serves by divine providence not as an obstacle to faith, but as its irrevocable empirical confirmation for all generations.

[^479]: Patristic imagery of the divine fire and the unconsumed bush (Exod. 3:2): the earthen mortal hand of Thomas touches the immaterial fire of Christ's glorified Divinity without being consumed, testifying to the union of Godhead and manhood in the one Person of Christ.

[^480]: The 1907 Lviv Stauropegion edition retains the statutory Austro-Hungarian imperial prayer (*І҆мпера́тора*), invoking divine protection for Apostolic Emperor Franz Joseph I (reigned 1848–1916), under whose imperial patronal sanction the Stauropegion Institute operated.

[^481]: *Tone 1, Podoben: "While the soldiers were guarding Thy tomb" (Гро́бъ тво́й Сп҃се)* is the classical Resurrectional Sessional melody *Τὸν τάφον σου Σωτήρ*, sung throughout the Paschal season.

[^482]: The Church Slavonic reading *не невѣ́рꙋй мѝ во́лче* ("do not disbelieve Me, O wolf-like one" or "O impetuous one") translates the Greek *μὴ ἀπίστει μοι λύκε* or represents a recensional variant addressing Thomas's fierce and relentless questioning, which Christ converts from predatory skepticism into apostolic witness.

[^483]: Theological comparison between Saint John the Theologian and Saint Thomas: John reclined upon Christ's breast at the Mystical Supper (John 13:23–25) to draw forth sublime theology, while Thomas was counted worthy to touch the resurrected ribs (John 20:26–28) to confirm the physical reality of the bodily Resurrection for the entire Church.

[^484]: *Tone 2, Podoben: "O House of Ephratha" (До́ме Еѵ҆фра́ѳовъ)* is the venerable Prosomoion melody *Οἶκος τοῦ Ἐφραθᾶ*, originally composed for the Forefeast of the Nativity of Christ and widely used for concise festal stichera across the Triodion and Pentecostarion.

[^485]: The Greek acrostic *Ὑμνῳδία Ἰωσήφ* ("The Hymnody of Joseph"), preserved in the printed Slavonic edition through marginal Greek and Slavonic letters marking the successive strophes of Odes 4, 8, and 9 of the Thursday Triodion composed by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886).

[^486]: *Tone 1, Podoben: "Joy of the heavenly hosts" (Небе́сныхъ чино́въ)* is the classical Prosomoion melody *Τῶν οὐρανίων ταγμάτων*, sung for festal stichera throughout the liturgical year.

[^487]: *Tone 1, Podoben: "When the stone had been sealed" (Ка́мени запеча́танꙋ)* is the venerable Prosomoion melody *Τοῦ λίθου σφραγισθέντος*, adapted here for the Friday Sessional Hymn of the Precious Cross.

[^488]: A Stavrotheotokion (*Крестобогоро́диченъ*) expressing the maternal lament of the Virgin Mary at the foot of the Cross, contemplating the paradox of the Creator hanging lifeless upon the Tree to effect mankind's salvation.

[^489]: *Tone 4, Podoben: "As noble among the martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ)* is the widely celebrated Prosomoion melody *Ὡς γενναῖον ἐν μάρτυσιν*, originally composed for the Great Martyr George and adapted here for the post-festal Paschal stichera of Thomas Week.

[^490]: The Greek acrostic *Δόξα τῷ Θεῷ ἀμήν* ("Glory to God, Amen"), preserved in the printed Slavonic edition through marginal Greek and Slavonic letters marking the successive strophes of Odes 5, 8, and 9 of the Friday Triodion composed by Saint Joseph the Hymnographer.

[^491]: A profound dogmatic formulation of the hypostatic union: touching the wounded side whence issued blood and water (John 19:34), Thomas confesses Christ to be neither "bare God" (*Бо́га не на́га*, without true human nature) nor "mere man" (*человѣ́ка не про́ста*, without true divinity), but the Incarnate Word, true God and true Man.

[^492]: The solemn Dogmatic Theotokion of Tone 1, *Всемі́рную сла́ву* ("The glory of all the world"), composed by Saint John of Damascus, celebrating the Virgin Mary as the heavenly gate, the temple of the Godhead, and the champion through whom the barrier of enmity was overthrown.

[^493]: The rubric specifies that throughout the Pentecostarion period, the Octoechos canons for the departed of the current tone are sung at Compline on Friday evenings rather than at Saturday Matins, thereby preserving the Paschal character of the Saturday morning office while fulfilling the weekly commemoration of the faithful departed.

[^494]: A Four-Ode Canon (Church Slavonic: Четверопѣ́снецъ; Greek: Τετραῴδιον / Tetraodion) of Thomas Saturday, consisting of Odes 6, 7, 8, and 9. In the Triodion and Pentecostarion traditions, weekday canons frequently employ such condensed four-ode structures.

[^495]: The acrostic Πρῶτον μέλπω μέλος ("I chant the first song") is formed by the initial Greek letters of the troparia across the four odes (Π, Ρ, Ω, Τ in Ode 6; Ο, Ν, Μ, Ε in Ode 7; Λ, Π, Ω, Μ in Ode 8; and Ε, Λ, Ο, Σ in Ode 9), designating this composition as the work of St. Joseph the Hymnographer.

[^496]: Prokeimenon of the Liturgy on Saturday of Thomas Week: Psalm 146:5, 1a (LXX) [Masoretic Psalm 147:5, 1].

[^497]: The Epistle reading for Saturday of the Second Week after Pascha (Thomas Week): Acts 5:21b-32 (pericope 15).

[^498]: The Gospel reading for Saturday of the Second Week after Pascha: John 6:14-27 (pericope 16).

[^499]: Typikon prescription establishing that this liturgical order for the Divine Liturgy (Beatitudes with Ode 9, Prokeimenon, Epistle, Alleluia, Gospel, and Communion verse) serves as the standard model for all Saturdays throughout the Paschal season up to the Apodosis of the Ascension.

[^500]: Codicological note: A decorative engraved headpiece depicting the Risen Christ appearing to the Holy Myrrh-bearing Women outside the open tomb, with an angel pointing toward the empty sepulcher, marking the major liturgical division for the Third Sunday after Pascha.

[^501]: The Third Sunday after Pascha commemorates the Holy Myrrh-bearing Women (Mary Magdalene, Mary the wife of Cleopas, Joanna, Salome the mother of the sons of Zebedee, Susanna, and Martha and Mary the sisters of Lazarus), together with Righteous Joseph of Arimathea and the secret disciple Nicodemus.

[^502]: Printed folio 283 corresponds to physical leaf p288 (`Page_0288.jpg`), where the full texts of the Tone 2 Resurrectional stichera are printed at Great Vespers.

[^503]: The prophetic citation of Ezekiel 44:2 ("This gate shall be shut, no man shall pass through it...") provides the foundational Old Testament typological witness for the Perpetual Virginity of the Theotokos (*semper virgo*), central to the Saturday evening Tone 2 Dogmatikon.

[^504]: Printed folio 284 corresponds to physical leaf p289 (`Page_0289.jpg`), where the Doxastikon for the Myrrh-bearing Women is printed in full at Great Vespers.

[^505]: Hymnographic attribution: Anatolius (А҆нато́лїа), patriarch of Constantinople (d. 458) or 9th-century Studite hymnographer, to whom these three idiomelon stichera for the Myrrh-bearers at "Lord, I have cried" are traditionally attributed.

[^506]: Hymnographic attribution: St. Cosmas the Monk (Козмы̀ мона́ха, St. Cosmas of Maiuma, d. c. 787), composer of this Tone 6 Doxastikon ("The myrrh-bearing women reached Thy tomb...").

[^507]: Printed folio 15 corresponds to physical leaf p20 (`Page_0020.jpg`), where the Paschal Stichera ("Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered...") are printed in full with their Paschal verses.

[^508]: The solemn Holy Saturday burial hymn (*Tebe odeyushchagosya svetom yako rizoyu* / "Thee Who clothest Thyself with light as with a garment"), sung here as the Doxastikon of the Aposticha; the Ruthenian liturgical tradition specifically preserves the canonical term *plashchanytsia* (shroud / winding sheet) for the embroidered cloth icon of the Entombment.

[^509]: The Typikon prescribes that from the Sunday of Saint Thomas through the feast of the Ascension, the customary distribution and consumption of the blessed bread and wine in the narthex (*artoklasia*) following Great Vespers is omitted, because of the brevity of the night hours during the spring and summer season.

[^510]: The 3rd Matins Resurrection Gospel (*Eothinon*): Mark 16:9-20 (pericope 71), narrating the appearance of the Risen Christ to Mary Magdalene and the two disciples on the road to Emmaus, followed by the Great Commission.

[^511]: The Canon for the Sunday of the Myrrh-bearing Women is the composition of St. Andrew, Archbishop of Crete (c. 660–740), the famed hymnographer and author of the Great Canon of Repentance.

[^512]: Printed folio 5 corresponds to physical leaf p10 (`Page_0010.jpg`), where Ode 1 of the Golden Paschal Canon of St. John of Damascus is printed in full.

[^513]: The acrostic of the companion Paschal Theotokion Canon forms the name of its author: ΘΕΟΦΑΝΗΣ (St. Theophanes the Branded / Graptos, c. 778–845, Metropolitan of Nicaea), whose troparia are appended to each ode of the Paschal canon.

[^514]: Printed folio 6 corresponds to physical leaf p11 (`Page_0011.jpg`), where Ode 3 of the Paschal Canon is printed in full.

[^515]: Printed folio 7 corresponds to physical leaf p12 (`Page_0012.jpg`), where the Paschal Kontakion ("Though Thou didst descend into the tomb...") and Ikos are printed in full.

[^516]: Printed folio 8 corresponds to physical leaf p13 (`Page_0013.jpg`), where Ode 4 of the Paschal Canon is printed in full.

[^517]: The Heirmos of Ode 4 draws directly upon the prophetic prayer of Habakkuk (Hab. 3:2-3 LXX: "God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the thick and shady mountain").

[^518]: Printed folio 9 corresponds to physical leaf p14 (`Page_0014.jpg`), where Ode 5 of the Paschal Canon is printed in full.

[^519]: Printed folio 10 corresponds to physical leaf p15 (`Page_0015.jpg`), where Ode 6 of the Paschal Canon is printed in full.

[^520]: The Synaxarion here references the *Acta Pilati* or *Gospel of Nicodemus*, an early Christian apocryphon containing a detailed narrative of the Trial, Passion, Entombment, and the Descent of Christ into Hades (*Harrowing of Hell*), traditionally attributed to the secret disciple Nicodemus.

[^521]: According to ancient Eastern Christian tradition, St. Mary Magdalene travelled to Rome and obtained an audience before Emperor Tiberius Caesar, presenting him with a red egg and boldly denouncing the unjust trial and condemnation of Christ by Pontius Pilate and the high priests Annas and Caiaphas.

[^522]: The translation of the relics of St. Mary Magdalene from Ephesus to Constantinople occurred in the year 899 under the Byzantine Emperor Leo VI the Wise (886–912), who enshrined them in the Monastery of St. Lazarus.

[^523]: Printed folio 11 corresponds to physical leaf p16 (`Page_0016.jpg`), where Ode 7 of the Paschal Canon is printed in full.

[^524]: Codicological note: In the source exemplar scan, physical leaf p300 corresponds to printed page 294 (fol. 294 / Сч҃д), following immediately after physical leaf p299 (printed page 291 / Сч҃а). Printed pages 292 (Сч҃в) and 293 (Сч҃г), which contained the conclusion of Ode 7, all of Ode 8, and the opening of Ode 9 of the Canon of the Myrrh-bearing Women, were omitted in the scan. Leaf p300 resumes directly in the middle of Ode 9 with the troparion "Thou wast buffeted for our sake..."

[^525]: Printed folio 14 corresponds to physical leaf p19 (`Page_0019.jpg`), where Ode 9 of the Paschal Canon and its Zadostoinik ("Shine, shine, O new Jerusalem...") are printed in full.

[^526]: The rubrical note specifies that the preceding two psalm verses from Psalm 9 (Ps. 9:33 and 9:2 LXX) serve as the standard final two stichera refrains (*zapivky*) at the Praises across all Sundays of the liturgical year whenever eight stichera are appointed.

[^527]: The 3rd Matins Evangelical Sticheron (*Eothinon*), composed by the Byzantine Emperor Leo VI the Wise (886–912), meditating on the third resurrectional Gospel pericope (Mark 16:9-20).

[^528]: The Catechesis of St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), Abbot of the Studion Monastery in Constantinople, traditionally read in the narthex (*prytvor*) following Sunday Matins during the Paschal season.

[^529]: Acts 6:1-7 (pericope 16) recounts the appointment of the first seven deacons (Stephen, Philip, Prochorus, Nicanor, Timon, Parmenas, and Nicholas), chosen to serve tables so the Apostles could devote themselves to prayer and the ministry of the Word.

[^530]: Mark 15:43–16:8 (pericope 69) details the petition of Joseph of Arimathea before Pilate, the burial of Christ in the rock-hewn tomb, and the visit of Mary Magdalene, Mary the mother of James, and Salome on the morning of the Resurrection.

[^531]: The alternating distribution of troparia during the week of the Myrrh-bearers reflects the ancient liturgical practice of commemorating St. Joseph of Arimathea and the holy Myrrh-bearers at daily Vespers and Matins, balancing the Resurrectional themes with their specific historical witnesses.

[^532]: Monday of the second week after Pascha corresponds to Monday of Thomas Week (see physical leaf p260, fol. 255), where the order of daily Paschal Matins without the Polyeleos is first detailed.

[^533]: The weekday Matins canon structure combines the feast's Paschal-Myrrhbearer canon with the canon from the daily Menaion, totaling 12 troparia per ode.

[^534]: The *Prologue* (Church Slavonic: Прологъ; Greek: Συναξάрион) is the traditional Slavonic collection of brief hagiographical lives and moral-edifying patristic narratives arranged by calendar day, read liturgically after Ode 6 of Matins.

[^535]: The Typikon prescribes the standard order of troparia and kontakia at the Divine Liturgy for ferias (weekdays) of the Myrrh-bearers, prioritizing the feast's three troparia followed by temple and Menaion saints, unless superseded by a ranked Polyeleos feast.

[^536]: The Three-Ode Canon (*Triodion*) for Monday of the Myrrh-bearers was composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886). Its Greek acrostic ᾍδει τάδε Ἰωσήф ('Joseph sings these') is distributed letter-by-letter across the troparia of Odes 1, 8, and 9.

[^537]: The rubric refers back to the general rule established on Monday of the Myrrh-bearers (physical leaf p304, fol. 298), directing the alternation of the three troparia ('The noble Joseph...', 'When Thou didst descend unto death...', and 'Unto the Myrrh-bearing women...') at 'God is the Lord' on all weekdays of this third week after Pascha.

[^538]: The Tone 2 Praises sticheron *Let the Jews say...* (Church Slavonic: Да реку́тъ І҆ꙋде́и; Greek: Εἰπάτωσαν Ἰουδαῖοι) directly refutes the polemic regarding the bribery of the Roman guard recorded in the Gospel of Matthew (Matt. 28:11–15), affirming the empty tomb as indisputable evidence of Christ's bodily Resurrection.

[^539]: The automelon *When from the Tree* (Church Slavonic: Є҆гда̀ ѿ дре́ва; Greek: Ὅτε ἐκ τοῦ ξύλου), composed by St. Theophanes the Branded (c. 778–845), originally served as the chief model for the Great Friday Vespers aposticha on the Deposition of Christ from the Cross by Joseph of Arimathea and Nicodemus; its melody governs numerous prosomoia throughout Paschaltide.

[^540]: The Tuesday Matins Three-Ode Canon (*Triodion*), composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), bears the Greek acrostic ΧΡΙΣΤΕ ΣΩΣΟΝ ΜΕ (*Christ, save me!*), which is distributed across the initial letters of the troparia in Odes 2, 8, and 9.

[^541]: The Tone 6 Doxasticon *Joseph asked for the Body of Jesus* (Church Slavonic: Просѝ І҆ѡ́сифъ Тѣ́ло І҆и҃сово; Greek: ᾘτήσαто Ἰωσὴφ τὸ σῶμα τοῦ Ἰησοῦ) is drawn from the classic Great Friday Vespers hymnography of St. Symeon the Metaphrast, celebrating the daring petition of the noble counselor before Pontius Pilate.

[^542]: The rubric refers back to the general weekly ordinance established on Monday of the Myrrh-bearers (physical leaf p304, fol. 298r), directing the daily alternation of the three feast troparia (*The noble Joseph...*, *When Thou didst descend unto death...*, and *Unto the Myrrh-bearing women...*) at 'God is the Lord' on all ferias of the third week after Pascha.

[^543]: The abbreviated rubric *Loving-kindness* (Church Slavonic: Бл҃гоꙋтро́бїа) designates the Tone 2 sessional automelon *Being the wellspring of loving-kindness* (Greek: Εὐσπλαγχνίας ὑπάρχουσα πηγή; Church Slavonic: Милосе́рдїѧ / Благоꙋтро́бїѧ сꙋ́щи и҆сто́чникъ), historically appointed for both festal and penitential sessional strophes.

[^544]: The monumental Tone 5 Aposticha Doxasticon *Thee Who clothest Thyself with light as with a garment* (Church Slavonic: Тебѐ Ѡ҆дѣ́ющагося свѣ́томъ ꙗ҆́кѡ ри́зою; Greek: Σὲ τὸν ἀναβαλλόμενον τὸ φῶς ὥσπερ ἱμάтиον) was composed by the 9th-century Byzantine hymnographer Kassiani (Nun Cassia); it forms the emotional climax of Great Friday Vespers, here repeated at Wednesday Matins of the Myrrh-bearers.

[^545]: The Wednesday Matins Three-Ode Canon (*Triodion*), composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), features the Greek acrostic Ἠγέρθη ὁ Σωτήр (*The Savior is risen!*), distributed across the troparia of Odes 3, 8, and 9.

[^546]: The Aposticha sticheron for the Apostles pairs the preeminent Apostles Peter and Paul: Peter called from the Galilean fishermen whose shadow healed the sick (Acts 5:15), and Paul called from among the Jewish Pharisees whose garments and aprons banished diseases and evil spirits (Acts 19:12).

[^547]: The sessional automelon *Most blessed art thou* (Church Slavonic: Пребл҃гослове́нна; Greek: Ὑπερευλογημένη ὑπάρχεις) is the celebrated festive Theotokion traditionally chanted before the Great Doxology at Sunday Matins, hailing the Virgin Mary through whose incarnate Son hades was despoiled and death slain.

[^548]: The Tone 2 Praises sticheron *An Angel, on the one hand, brought the "Rejoice!"* (Church Slavonic: А҆́гг҃лъ оу҆́бѡ є҆́же ра́дꙋйсѧ; Greek: Ὁ Ἄγγελος μὲν τὸ Χαῖρε) juxtaposes the Archangel Gabriel's Annunciation to the Mother of God with the Angel rolling away the stone at the empty tomb, contrasting the sorrow of Eve with the joy of the Resurrection. In the 1907 Lviv imprint, a printer's set-off error or frisket misalignment accidentally impressed the final clauses of this sticheron into the top margin above the running header on fol. 316r (physical leaf p321).

[^549]: The Thursday Matins Three-Ode Canon (*Triodion*), composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), bears the Greek acrostic ᾎ́SMA ᾌDW TW ΘEW (*A song I sing unto God*), distributed across the troparia of Odes 7, 8, and 9.

[^550]: The Tone 8 automelon *O marvelous wonder* (Church Slavonic: Ѽ пресла́внагѡ чꙋдесѐ; Greek: Ὢ τοῦ παραδόξου θαύματος), composed by St. John of Damascus (c. 676–749), serves as the prime melodic model for festive aposticha and praises across the Byzantine liturgical cycle.

[^551]: The rubric refers back to the general weekly ordinance established on Monday of the Myrrh-bearers (physical leaf p304, fol. 298r), directing the daily alternation of the three feast troparia (*The noble Joseph...*, *When Thou didst descend unto death...*, and *Unto the Myrrh-bearing women...*) at 'God is the Lord' on all ferias of the third week after Pascha.

[^552]: The venerable Tone 2 sessional hymn *We venerate Thine immaculate icon, O Good One* (Church Slavonic: Пречⷭ҇томꙋ ѻ҆́бразꙋ твоемꙋ̀ покланѧ́емсѧ Бл҃гі́й; Greek: Τὴν ἄχранτον εἰκόνα σου προσκυνοῦμεν ἀγαθέ) is one of the classic dogmatic troparia of the Byzantine rite defending the veneration of holy images against the iconoclasts, appointed here on Friday in direct connection with the contemplation of Christ's voluntary Crucifixion.

[^553]: The Friday Matins Three-Ode Canon (*Triodion*), composed by St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), bears the Greek acrostic ΔΟΞΑ ΘΕΩ ΑΜΗΝ (*Glory to God, amen*), distributed across Odes 5, 8, and 9.

[^554]: The Tone 6 Doxasticon *The Myrrh-bearing women, having reached Thy tomb* (Church Slavonic: Мѵроно́сицы жєны̀, гро́ба твоегѡ̀ дости́гша; Greek: Αἱ Μυроφόροι γυναῖκες τοῦ μνήματος ἐπέστησαν) articulates the bewildered lament of the women disciples, who feared that their hope had been stolen away before the reality of the Resurrection was revealed.

[^555]: The monumental Tone 5 Aposticha Doxasticon *Thee Who clothest Thyself with light as with a garment* (Church Slavonic: Тебѐ Ѡ҆дѣ́ющагося свѣ́томъ ꙗ҆́кѡ ри́зою; Greek: Σὲ τὸν ἀναβαλλόμενον τὸ φῶς ὥσπερ ἱμάτιον) was composed by the 9th-century Byzantine hymnographer Kassiani (Nun Cassia); it forms the liturgical and musical culmination of Great Friday Vespers, repeated here at Friday Vespers of the Myrrh-bearers.

[^556]: The closing rubric (*In this manner the service is performed also on all Fridays of Pentecost*) establishes the structural norm for Friday Vespers throughout the fifty days of Paschaltide, where the Saturday commemoration of the Resurrection and the holy martyrs is prepared by combining festal Paschal hymnography with the Friday evening psalmody.

[^557]: The typikon schema assigns 14 troparia total: 6 for the Pentecostarion canon of the Holy Myrrh-bearing Women, 4 for the Patron Saint of the temple, and 4 for the daily commemoration from the Menaion.

[^558]: This idiomel sticheron (Greek: Ἦλθον ἐπὶ τὸ μνῆμα ἡ Μαγδαληνὴ) serves as the morning Doxastikon of the Praises for the Apodosis Saturday of the Myrrh-bearers, summarizing the Gospel witness of Matthew 28:1–7.

[^559]: The Four-Ode Canon (Tetraode, comprising Odes 6, 7, 8, and 9) for Saturday of the Myrrh-bearing Women is a work of St. Joseph the Hymnographer, chanted at Matins alongside the primary festal canon.

[^560]: Rubric: At the Divine Liturgy on the Apodosis Saturday of the Holy Myrrh-bearing Women, the Beatitudes (Makarismoi) are taken from Ode 9 of the festal canon to 6 troparia.

[^561]: Acts, Pericope 22 (Acts 9:19–31), recording the preaching of Saul in Damascus, his escape over the wall, and his reception by the Apostles in Jerusalem.

[^562]: John, Pericope 52 (John 15:17–16:2), containing the Lord's discourse on the world's hatred and the coming of the Comforter.

[^563]: Internal cross-reference to printed folio 330 (Physical Leaf p336), where these stichera are printed in full at Great Vespers.

[^564]: Melodic model incipit: Greek Μεγάλη τοῦ Σταυροῦ σου Κύριε ἡ δύναμις / Church Slavonic Ве́лїѧ крⷭ҇та̀ твоегѡ̀, Гдⷭ҇и, си́ла ("Great is the power of Thy Cross, O Lord"), taken from the Saturday Vespers stichera of the Octoechos in Tone 3.

[^565]: Cross-reference to printed folio 332 (Physical Leaf p338), where this sticheron is printed in full at the Aposticha of Great Vespers.

[^566]: Cross-reference to printed folio 65 (Physical Leaf p71), where the Paschal Stichera ("Let God arise...") are printed in full.

[^567]: Typikon rubric for Small/Daily Midnight Office when an All-Night Vigil is not celebrated: the Triadic Canon (Canon to the Holy Trinity) is taken from the Octoechos in Tone 3.

[^568]: Rubric: After the Evlogetaria of the Resurrection ("Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes... The assembly of the Angels was amazed..."), the Hypakoe of Tone 3 is sung.

[^569]: Psalm 95:10 (LXX 95:10), the Resurrection prokeimenon of the Sunday Matins service in Tone 3.

[^570]: Luke, Pericope 114 (Luke 24:1–12), the 4th Resurrection Matins Gospel, narrating the arrival of the myrrh-bearing women at the sepulchre, the appearance of the two angels, and Peter running to the empty tomb.

[^571]: Internal cross-reference to printed folio 320 (Physical Leaf p325), where the Theotokia of the Paschal Canon are printed in full on the Sunday of the Holy Myrrh-bearing Women.

[^572]: The troparia addressed to the Archangel Michael in the Canon of the Paralytic (Tone 3) reflect the ancient patristic and liturgical tradition identifying Michael as the angel who descended yearly to trouble the water of the Sheep Pool (Bethesda) for miraculous healing (cf. John 5:4).

[^573]: Habakkuk 3:3 (LXX): "God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from the mountain overshadowed and densely wooded."

[^574]: Jonah 2:1–11 (LXX): The canticle of the Prophet Jonah from the belly of the sea beast, the scriptural typological archetype for Ode 6 and Christ's three-day burial.

[^575]: The Synaxarion for the Fourth Sunday after Pascha, composed by Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–c. 1335), explaining the biblical and typikon rationale for celebrating the miracle of the Paralytic at Bethesda (John 5:1–15) during the Paschal Pentecostarion cycle.

[^576]: Daniel 3:49–52 (LXX): The Prayer of the Three Holy Children delivered from the Babylonian furnace, the scriptural basis for Ode 7.

[^577]: Daniel 3:57–88 (LXX): The Hymn of the Three Holy Children ("Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord"), the scriptural foundation for Ode 8.

[^578]: Ode 9 unites the biblical theophanies of the burning bush seen by Moses (Exodus 3:2), the unhewn mountain seen by Daniel (Daniel 2:34–35, 45), and the budding rod of Jesse prophesied by Isaiah (Isaiah 11:1), all fulfilled in the Virgin Theotokos.

[^579]: The Eastern or Anatolian Resurrection stichera (Ἀνατολικὰ Σтиχηρά), historically attributed in liturgical collections to Patriarch Anatolius of Constantinople (d. 458).

[^580]: The 4th Resurrection Gospel Sticheron (Eothinon / ἑωθινόν) in Tone 3, authored by Emperor Leo VI the Wise (886–912), meditating on the fourth Matins Gospel pericope (Luke 24:1–12).

[^581]: Rubric prescribing the reading of the ascetic and monastic catechetical homilies of St. Theodore the Studite (759–826), appointed for Sundays throughout the Triodion and Pentecostarion seasons.

[^582]: Psalm 32:22 (LXX 32:22): Prokeimenon at the Divine Liturgy in Tone 1.

[^583]: Acts, Pericope 23 (Acts 9:32–42), recounting the healing of Aeneas the paralytic at Lydda and the raising of Tabitha (Dorcas) from the dead at Joppa through the prayers of the Apostle Peter.

[^584]: Psalm 88:2 (LXX 88:2): Alleluia verses chanted before the Holy Gospel at the Divine Liturgy in Tone 5.

[^585]: John, Pericope 14 (John 5:1–15), the Gospel reading of the Divine Liturgy on the Fourth Sunday after Pascha, recounting the healing of the paralytic of thirty-eight years at Bethesda on the Sabbath day.

[^586]: Typikon rubric governing the post-festal period of the Paralytic: the liturgical office of the miracle continues through Monday and concludes with its apodosis (Apodosis) on Tuesday, immediately preceding the feast of Mid-Pentecost on Wednesday.

[^587]: Psalm 122:1–2 (LXX 122:1–2): The first verse of the Aposticha at Sunday Vespers.

[^588]: Psalm 122:3 (LXX 122:3): The second verse of the Aposticha at Sunday Vespers.

[^589]: Typikon rubric governing daily Matins on Monday of the Paralytic: when an All-Night Vigil is not celebrated, the Sunday Resurrection troparion in Tone 3 is repeated twice at "God is the Lord," followed by the commemoration of the daily Menaion saint.

[^590]: Psalm 6:2 (LXX) / Psalm 6:1 (MT).

[^591]: Psalm 67:36 (LXX) / Psalm 68:35 (MT).

[^592]: The Canon, Kontakion, and Exapostilarion of the Paralytic are found on Sunday of the Paralytic (see fols. 327–328 / Physical Leaves p341–p344).

[^596]: In the printed Slavonic text, the initial Greek letters of the acrostic Ἀνέστη ὁ Σωτήρ are marked in the margin: *A*, *N*, *E*, *S* (Ode 1); *T*, *I*, *O*, *S* (Ode 8); and *Ō*, *T*, *I*, *R* (Ode 9).

[^597]: On Monday evening of the Paralytic, at *Lord, I have cried*, 6 stichera are appointed: 2 idiomela of the feast (repeating the first) and 3 from the Menaion.

[^608]: Mid-Pentecost (*Преполове́нїе Пѧтдесѧ́тницы*) is celebrated for eight days, standing midway between Pascha and the Descent of the Holy Spirit (Pentecost).

[^609]: Composite liturgical paremia: Micah 4:2–3, 4:5; 6:2–5, 6:8; 5:4 (LXX).

[^610]: The Slavonic text prints *[помѧни́те оу҆́бо] что̀ совѣща́ша на ва́съ и҆ сопроти́внїи* ("remember now what they took counsel against you, even the adversaries"), abbreviating the names of Balak king of Moab and Balaam son of Beor found in the full Septuagint text of Micah 6:5.

[^1017]: Matins Canon distribution rubric: Canon of the Samaritan Woman chanted with its Heirmoi to 8, and the Canon of the Menaion of the day to 4. Physical Leaf p421 (printed fol. 415r).

[^1018]: Matins hymnographic rubric prescribing the Kontakion and Ikos of the Samaritan Woman (from Ode 6) and the Exapostilarion of the Samaritan Woman following Ode 9. Physical Leaf p421 (printed fol. 415r).

[^1019]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4 (Resurrectional from the Octoechos), chanted twice. Physical Leaf p421 (printed fol. 415r). Confessing the undivided incarnation of the only-begotten Word of God and His voluntary Passion in the flesh while remaining impassible in His divine nature.

[^1020]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4 (Octoechos), second sticheron. Physical Leaf p421 (printed fol. 415r). The printed Church Slavonic text contains the typographical slip Пекни́жныѧ for Некни́жныѧ (unlettered / illiterate, Greek ἀγραμμάτους), celebrating the Holy Spirit's empowerment of the unlettered Apostles to confound worldly deceit through diverse tongues.

[^1021]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4: Martyrikon (*Мꙋ́чениченъ*). Physical Leaf p421 (printed fol. 415r). Praising the holy Martyrs who, encompassed with mortal body, vanquished bodiless adversaries and remained undaunted by the tyrants' threats and torments.

[^1022]: Stichera at the Praises: Doxastikon, Tone 2 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*). Physical Leaf p421 (printed fol. 415r). Hymnographic depiction of the dialogue at Jacob's well between Christ the Savior and the Samaritan woman, recounting His prophetic disclosure of her five past husbands and current unlawful consort (Jn 4:16–18, 29).

[^1023]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ): first sticheron, concluded from p421. Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). Extolling the divine condescension of Christ in conversing with the Samaritan woman to grant remission and salvation unto all mankind.

[^1024]: Psalm verse accompanying the second Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 44:5 [LXX 44:5 / MT 45:4]: "Bend Thy bow, and proceed prosperously, and reign, for the sake of truth, and meekness, and righteousness." Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v).

[^1025]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ): second sticheron. Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). The Samaritan woman's testimony in Sychar, declaring Christ to be the supreme Prophet Who revealed all her secret deeds, and imploring living water unto eternal life (Jn 4:14, 29).

[^1026]: Psalm verse accompanying the third Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 44:8 [LXX 44:8 / MT 45:7]: "Thou hast loved righteousness, and hated iniquity." Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v).

[^1027]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ): third sticheron. Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). Christ, the ever-flowing Fountain and immortal Life, sits beside the well while the disciples seek nourishment, snaring the Samaritan woman to enlighten her spiritual eyes.

[^1028]: Stichera at the Aposticha: Doxastikon, Tone 3 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*). Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). Cosmic hymn of joy celebrating the Incarnation of Christ, Who clothes the clouds with waters yet condescends to ask a cup of water from the Samaritan woman in order to free humanity from the ancestral curse.

[^1029]: Matins Three-Ode Canon (Triodion) for Thursday of the Samaritan Woman, attributed to St. Joseph the Hymnographer. Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). The rubric identifies the alphabetic acrostic in Greek: *ᾨδὴ Θεῷ πρέπει* ("A song befits God"), transcribed in Slavonic characters as Ѽдѣ̀ Ѳеѡ̀ прѣ́пеі, сі́есть: Пѣ́снь Бг҃ꙋ подоба́етъ.

[^1030]: Thursday Triodion, Tone 4, Ode 4: Heirmos (*Любвѐ ра́ди Ще́дре твоегѡ̀ ѡ҆́браза*). Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). Based on Habakkuk 3:2–19, celebrating Christ standing upon the Cross out of love for His image (mankind), whereby the heathen nations melted with awe.

[^1031]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 4: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial Ὠ (Ѡ). Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). The cosmic trembling of the earth, mountains, and hills upon witnessing the voluntary crucifixion of the Long-suffering Lord.

[^1032]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 4: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial δ (д). Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). Hades terrorized by the descent of the Savior, compelled to yield up the imprisoned dead who hymn His ineffable power.

[^1033]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 4: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ὴ (И). Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). Denunciation of the ungrateful Jewish council that condemned unto death the Author of Life and Resurrection, Who had opened the eyes of the blind.

[^1034]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 4: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial Θ (Ѳ), completing the word ᾨδὴ Θ-. Physical Leaf p422 (printed fol. 416v). Commemorating the Mother of God rejoicing with the Apostles upon beholding her Son arisen from the tomb.

[^1035]: Thursday Triodion, Tone 4, Ode 8: Heirmos (*Всѧ̑ дѣла̀ Бж҃їѧ, и҆ всѧ̀ тва́рь, бл҃гослови́те Гдⷭ҇а*). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). Derived from the Song of the Three Holy Children (Daniel 3:57–88 [LXX]), summoning all creation to bless the Lord Who brought radiant light and eternal life unto the world from the earth.

[^1036]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 8: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial ε (Є). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). The confession of the wise thief upon the cross, beholding the divine power and dread dispensation of the Master (Lk 23:42).

[^1037]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 8: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial ω (Ѡ). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). Christ's merciful greeting ("Rejoice!") unto the Myrrh-bearing women, transforming the primordial sorrow of Eve into joy through His Resurrection.

[^1038]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 8: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial π (П). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). Petition unto Jesus the Giver of light, Who healed the man blind from birth (Jn 9:1–7), to open the eyes of the chanter's heart to comprehend His divine precepts.

[^1039]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 8: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ρ (Р), completing -εῷ πρ-. Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). Supplication unto the all-pure Virgin for deliverance from Gehenna and eternal condemnation, directing the soul unto the serene haven of life.

[^1040]: Thursday Triodion, Tone 4, Ode 9: Heirmos (*Ка́мень нерꙋкосѣ́чный ѿ несѣко́мыѧ горы̀*). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). Typological interpretation of Daniel 2:34, 45, celebrating the virginal conception of Christ the cornerstone Who unified heavenly and earthly natures.

[^1041]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 9: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial έ (Є). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). Christ stretching forth His holy hands upon the Cross to heal the destructive wound caused by Adam's outstretched hand unto the forbidden tree (Gen 3:6).

[^1042]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 9: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial π (П). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). Shattering the gates and bars of death, and despoiling the kingdoms of hades through the third-day Resurrection.

[^1043]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 9: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ε (Є). Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). The risen Christ's apostolic mandate to traverse the world in faith and preach the dread and ineffable mysteries (cf. Mt 28:19; Mk 16:15).

[^1044]: Thursday Triodion, Ode 9: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ι (І), concluding the acrostic ᾨδὴ Θεῷ πρέπει. Physical Leaf p423 (printed fol. 417r). The Virgin Mother rejoicing alongside the Myrrh-bearing women upon witnessing the risen Savior. The leaf concludes with a decorative typographical vignette featuring the Christological monogram A + Ω.

[^1045]: Vespers of Thursday of the Samaritan Woman, Stichera at "Lord, I have cried," Tone 4, Podoben: "O Lord, having ascended the Cross" (Гдⷭ҇и возше́дъ на крⷭ҇тъ): first sticheron. Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v). The Samaritan woman heralds Christ in Sychar as the long-expected Messiah foretold by Moses in the Torah (Deut 18:15–18; Jn 4:25–29).

[^1046]: Stichera at "Lord, I have cried," Tone 4, Podoben: "O Lord, having ascended the Cross" (Гдⷭ҇и возше́дъ на крⷭ҇тъ): second sticheron. Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v). The citizens of Sychar haste unto Jacob's well after tasting the sweetness of the woman's report, yearning to behold the inexhaustible Fountain Who refreshes parched souls.

[^1047]: Stichera at "Lord, I have cried," Tone 4, Podoben: "O Lord, having ascended the Cross" (Гдⷭ҇и возше́дъ на крⷭ҇тъ): third sticheron. Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v). The Samaritans confess that they believe not merely on the woman's testimony, but because they have personally recognized Christ as the true Savior of the world (Jn 4:42).

[^1048]: Rubric governing the Menaion stichera at "Lord, I have cried": 3 stichera are chanted from the Menaion of the day to complete the prescribed 6. Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v).

[^1049]: Stichera at "Lord, I have cried": Doxastikon, Tone 6 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*). Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v). The paradoxical mystery of the Creator Who hangs the earth upon the waters and clothes the sky in clouds, begging water of a sinful Samaritan woman at Jacob's well to rescue her from demonic ensnarement and ignite her heart with living water.

[^1050]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4: Cross-sticheron (*Крⷭ҇тона̀*). Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v). Exalting the Holy Cross as an invincible weapon that easily crushes the snares of the adversary.

[^1051]: Psalm verse accompanying the Resurrection Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 122:1–2 [LXX 122:1–2 / MT 123:1–2]: "Unto Thee have I lifted up mine eyes, O Thou that dwellest in heaven: behold, as the eyes of servants look unto the hands of their masters..." Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v).

[^1052]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4: Resurrectional sticheron (*Воскрⷭ҇на*). Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v). Christ ascending the Cross to annul the ancestral curse of Adam, descending into hades to liberate the primordial captives, and bestowing incorruption upon mankind.

[^1053]: Psalm verse accompanying the Martyrikon: Psalm 122:3–4 [LXX 122:3–4 / MT 123:3–4]: "Have mercy on us, O Lord, have mercy on us; for we are utterly filled with contempt..." Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v).

[^1054]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4: Martyrikon (*Мꙋ́чениченъ*). Physical Leaf p424 (printed fol. 418v). Addressing the holy Martyrs as living, rational sacrifices and unblemished sheep of Christ, petitioning that the faithful may be shepherded alongside them beside the still waters of eternal rest (cf. Ps 22:2 [LXX 22:2 / MT 23:2]).

[^1055]: Thursday Vespers Aposticha: Doxastikon, Tone 6 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*). Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r). Concluding Thursday Vespers with Christ's evangelical words unto the Samaritan woman: "If thou knewest the gift of God, and Who it is that saith to thee, Give Me to drink; thou wouldest have asked of Him, and He would have given thee living water" (Jn 4:10).

[^1056]: Friday of the Samaritan Woman, Matins opening rubric: the Resurrection Troparion of Tone 4 (*Светлꙋю воскресенїѧ проповедь*) is chanted at "God is the Lord". Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r).

[^1057]: Sessional Hymn following the First Stichology, Tone 4, Podoben: "Speedily anticipate" (Ско́рѡ предварѝ). Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r). Meditating on the voluntary crucifixion of Christ by the Jews, His outstretched hands drawing the Gentiles unto salvation, and His pierced side pouring out grace.

[^1058]: Psalm verse accompanying the Martyrikon: Psalm 67:36 [LXX 67:36 / MT 68:35]: "Marvelous is God in His saints, the God of Israel." Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r).

[^1059]: Sessional Hymn following the First Stichology, Tone 4: Martyrikon (*Мꙋ́чениченъ*). Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r). Commemorating the martyrs who received incorruptible crowns through Christ's divine power, vanquishing earthly persecutors and shattering demonic arrogance.

[^1060]: Sessional Hymn following the First Stichology, Tone 4: Cross-Theotokion (*Крⷭ҇тобг҃оро́диченъ*). Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r). The lamentation of the All-pure Virgin Mother standing at the foot of the Cross, marveling how the very Life of all could taste of death to revitalize mortal humanity.

[^1061]: Sessional Hymn following the Second Stichology, Tone 4, Podoben: "Speedily anticipate" (Ско́рѡ предварѝ). Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r). The Samaritan woman arriving at Jacob's well to draw physical water, encountering Christ the living Spring Who refreshes those scorched by the passions of this world.

[^1062]: Sessional Hymn following the Second Stichology: Doxology rubric indicating the repetition of the festal Sessional Hymn after "Glory... both now...". Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r).

[^1063]: Standard Matins Paschal rubric prescribing the hymn "Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ" (*Воскресе́нїе Хрїсто́во ви́дѣвше*) and Psalm 50 [LXX 50 / MT 51]. Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r).

[^1064]: Canon distribution rubric for Friday Matins: Canon of the Samaritan Woman with its Heirmos to 8, and the Canon of the Menaion to 4. Physical Leaf p425 (printed fol. 419r).

[^1065]: Friday Matins, Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4 (Octoechos), chanted twice. Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). Celebrating the Holy Cross as the divine banner given unto them that fear God (Ps 59:6 [LXX 59:6 / MT 60:4]), overthrowing the powers of darkness and restoring man unto ancient blessedness.

[^1066]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4 (Octoechos), second sticheron. Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). Entreating Christ the Savior, by His Cross and Resurrection, to guide the faithful unto truth, rescue them from enemy snares, and raise them from the fall of sin.

[^1067]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4: Martyrikon (*Мꙋ́чениченъ*). Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). Citing Psalm 115:6 [LXX 115:6 / MT 116:15] ("Precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of His saints"), praising the passion-bearers who endured sword, fire, and ice for the hope of celestial rewards.

[^1068]: Stichera at the Praises: Doxastikon, Tone 6 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*). Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). Depicting Christ, the Source of Life, gently overcoming the ethnic barrier of the Samaritan woman with gracious words and guiding her to seek eternal, living water.

[^1069]: Friday Matins, Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ): first sticheron. Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). The Samaritan woman leaving her waterpot at Jacob's well to hasten into Sychar, having discovered the spring of immortality that cools the passions of her heart (Jn 4:28).

[^1070]: Psalm verse accompanying the second Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 44:5 [LXX 44:5 / MT 45:4]: "Bend Thy bow, and proceed prosperously, and reign, for the sake of truth, and meekness, and righteousness." Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v).

[^1071]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ): second sticheron. Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). Christ confirming the woman's truthfulness regarding her lack of a lawful spouse, prompting her enthusiastic proclamation in the city of the Prophet Who revealed her hidden life.

[^1072]: Psalm verse accompanying the third Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 44:8 [LXX 44:8 / MT 45:7]: "Thou hast loved righteousness, and hated iniquity." Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v).

[^1073]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ): third sticheron. Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). Contrast between the corruptible well of Jacob and the divine stream of Christ entering the heart of the Samaritan woman, bedewing the burning heat of worldly passions.

[^1074]: Stichera at the Aposticha: Doxastikon, Tone 8 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*). Physical Leaf p426 (printed fol. 420v). Full text of the Aposticha Doxastikon, recounting the Samaritan woman's abandonment of her waterpot and her urgent appeal to the citizens of Sychar to behold the Knower of hearts (Jn 4:28–29).

[^1075]: Matins Three-Ode Canon (Triodion) for Friday of the Samaritan Woman, attributed to St. Joseph the Hymnographer. Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). The heading identifies the Greek acrostic *Δόξα τῷ Θεῷ ἀμήν* ("Glory to God, Amen"), rendered in Slavonic transliteration as До́ѯа тѡ̀ Ѳеѡ̀ а҆ми́н, сі́есть: Сла́ва Бг҃ꙋ, а҆ми́нь.

[^1076]: Friday Triodion, Tone 4, Ode 5: Heirmos (*Оу҆жасо́шасѧ всѧ́чєскаѧ ѡ҆ Бж҃ественнѣй сла́вѣ твое́й*). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). Isaiah 26:9, praising the wonder of the Virgin's womb containing the transcendent God and giving birth to the timeless Son.

[^1077]: Friday Triodion, Ode 5: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial Δ (Д). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). Christ bound and nailed unto the Wood of the Cross to untie humanity from the shackles of sin and fasten souls unto His love.

[^1078]: Friday Triodion, Ode 5: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial ό (О). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). Adam rejoicing in hades upon Christ's appearance, freed from his ancient sentence and ancestral chains.

[^1079]: Friday Triodion, Ode 5: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ξ (Ѯ). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). The Angel appearing within the empty sepulchre unto the Myrrh-bearing women, proclaiming that Christ is risen, having slain death.

[^1080]: Friday Triodion, Ode 5: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial α (А), completing Δόξα (ДОѮА). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). The Mother of God beholding the risen Lord emerging from the tomb radiant as a bridegroom from his bridal chamber.

[^1081]: Friday Triodion, Tone 4, Ode 8: Heirmos (*Ѻ҆́троки бл҃гочести́выѧ въ пещѝ*). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). Typology of the Three Youths in the Babylonian furnace (Dan 3), prefiguring the virginal birth and awakening the universe to praise the Lord.

[^1082]: Friday Triodion, Ode 8: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial τ (Т). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). Christ rebuilding human nature fallen in Adam by stretching out His hands upon the Tree and submitting to the piercing of His side.

[^1083]: Friday Triodion, Ode 8: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial ῷ (Ѡ). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). Hades terror-stricken by Christ's descent into its depths, compelled to surrender all the captive dead who unite in blessing the Lord.

[^1084]: Friday Triodion, Ode 8: third troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial Θ (Ѳ). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). The Angel sitting upon the tombstone directing the weeping Myrrh-bearers to proclaim the Resurrection unto the disciples.

[^1085]: Friday Triodion, Ode 8: Troparion to the Holy Trinity (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ε (Є). Physical Leaf p427 (printed fol. 421r). Doxological confession of the consubstantial Trinity in three hypostases: the unoriginate Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.

[^1086]: Friday Triodion, Ode 8: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ῷ (Ѡ), completing τῷ Θεῷ (ТѠ ѲЄѠ). Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). The Mother of God rejoicing in the Resurrection of Christ, Who arose leaving the sepulchral seals intact just as He preserved her virginity in childbirth.

[^1087]: Friday Triodion, Tone 4, Ode 9: Heirmos (*Всѧ́къ земноро́дный да взыгра́етсѧ Дх҃омъ просвѣща́емь*). Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). Universal festal summons for mortal men and bodiless angelic intellects to join in glorifying the all-holy Mother of God.

[^1088]: Friday Triodion, Ode 9: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial ἀ (А). Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). Christ voluntarily ascending the height of the Cross to raise humanity from the pit of destruction, and entering the tomb to awaken the dead.

[^1089]: Friday Triodion, Ode 9: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial μ (М). Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). The holy Myrrh-bearing women bringing spices unto the Author of life, and running to console the mourning Apostles with news of the Resurrection.

[^1090]: Friday Triodion, Ode 9: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ή (И). Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). Christ the radiant Sun illuminating the darkness of hades with His divine Soul, liberating the souls bound from the ages.

[^1091]: Friday Triodion, Ode 9: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ν (Н), concluding the acrostic Δόξα τῷ Θεῷ ἀμήν. Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). Intercessory supplication unto the Virgin who bore God Incarnate, that He may enlighten the hearts of all who magnify her.

[^1092]: Friday of the Samaritan Woman, Vespers stichera at "Lord, I have cried," Tone 1: first sticheron. Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). Commemorating Christ's arrival at Jacob's well at the sixth hour to redeem Eve's fruit, since at that exact hour Eve had departed from Paradise through the serpent's deceit (cf. Gen 3:1–6; Jn 4:6).

[^1093]: Stichera at "Lord, I have cried," Tone 2: second sticheron. Physical Leaf p428 (printed fol. 422v). The Samaritan woman petitioning Christ for the water of faith, anticipating the regenerative waters of holy Baptism.

[^1094]: Friday Vespers, Stichera at "Lord, I have cried": third sticheron, Tone 2. Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r). Commemorating the dialogue at the fountain, where the co-unoriginate Word of the Father revealed the Samaritan woman's marital history, leading her to proclaim Him unto the multitudes.

[^1095]: Friday Vespers, Stichera at "Lord, I have cried": Doxastikon, Tone 6 (*Сла́ва*). Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r). Hymnographic depiction of the divine humility of Christ, Who is enthroned upon the Cherubim and stretches out the sky with clouds, asking water of a Samaritan woman to extinguish the fire of her sins with living water.

[^1096]: Friday Vespers, Stichera at "Lord, I have cried": Dogmatikon Theotokion, Tone 4 (*И҆́же тебѐ ра́ди, Бг҃оѻте́цъ Прⷪ҇ро́къ Дв҃дъ*). Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r). Dogmatic exposition by St. John of Damascus based on Psalm 44:10–12 [LXX 44:10–12 / MT 45:9–11], celebrating the Virgin Mother as Queen standing at the right hand of God, through whom Christ assumed human nature to seek and save the lost sheep.

[^1097]: Friday Vespers, Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4 (Resurrectional from the Octoechos): first sticheron. Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r). Venerating the life-giving Cross of Christ and His third-day Resurrection, which renovated corrupt human nature and reopened the path to heaven.

[^1098]: Psalm verse accompanying the second Resurrection Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 92:1 [LXX 92:1 / MT 93:1]: "The Lord is King, He is clothed with majesty." Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r).

[^1099]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4 (Octoechos): second sticheron. Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r). Christ nailed unto the Cross to annul the ancestral transgression of the forbidden tree, descending into hades to burst the bonds of death.

[^1100]: Psalm verse accompanying the third Resurrection Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 92:1 [LXX 92:1 / MT 93:1]: "For He hath established the world, which shall not be moved." Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r).

[^1101]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4 (Octoechos): third sticheron. Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r). Christ destroying the kingdom of death by His own death, granting incorruptibility and eternal life unto the world.

[^1102]: Psalm verse accompanying the fourth Resurrection Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 92:5 [LXX 92:5 / MT 93:5]: "Holiness befits Thy house, O Lord, unto length of days." Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r).

[^1103]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4 (Octoechos): fourth sticheron. Physical Leaf p429 (printed fol. 423r). Praising the Savior Who ascended the Cross to wipe out the primordial condemnation and descended into hades to free those held captive from ancient ages.

[^1104]: Friday Vespers, Stichera at the Aposticha: Doxastikon, Tone 8 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*). Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v). The Samaritan woman leaving her water vessel at the well to announce unto the inhabitants of Sychar the arrival of the Knower of hearts, Christ the Messiah.

[^1105]: Saturday of the Samaritan Woman, Matins opening rubric: the Resurrection Troparion of Tone 4 (*Светлꙋю воскресенїѧ проповедь*) is chanted at "God is the Lord". Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v).

[^1106]: Sessional Hymn following the First Stichology, Tone 4 (Resurrectional), chanted twice. Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v). The holy Myrrh-bearing women trembling at the tomb entrance before the dazzling brilliance of the Angel, marveling whether the Lord Who opened Paradise unto the thief had risen from the dead.

[^1107]: Sessional Hymn following the First Stichology, Tone 4: Theotokion (*Бг҃оро́диченъ*). Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v). Confessing the mystery hidden from eternity and unknown even to angelic orders: God Incarnate in unconfused union, willingly submitting to the Cross to redeem Adam and save mankind from death.

[^1108]: Sessional Hymn following the Second Stichology, Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross" (Вознесы́йсѧ на крⷭ҇тъ). Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v). Cosmic rejoicing at Christ's nativity from the Virgin and His destruction of corruption through His death, shining with miracles unto the Samaritan woman and bestowing the fountain of healing.

[^1109]: Sessional Hymn following the Second Stichology: Doxology rubric indicating the repetition of the Sessional Hymn after "Glory... both now...". Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v).

[^1110]: Standard Matins Paschal rubric prescribing the hymn "Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ" (*Воскресе́нїе Хрїсто́во ви́дѣвше*) and Psalm 50 [LXX 50 / MT 51]. Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v).

[^1111]: Canon distribution rubric for Saturday Matins: Canon of the Samaritan Woman to 6, Canon of the Monastery's Patron Saint (*ст҃а́гѡ ѡ҆би́тели*) to 4, and Canon of the Menaion to 4. Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v).

[^1112]: Matins hymnographic rubric prescribing the Kontakion of the Samaritan Woman (chanted after Ode 6) and the Exapostilarion of the Samaritan Woman (chanted after Ode 9). Physical Leaf p430 (printed fol. 424v).

[^1113]: Saturday of the Samaritan Woman, Matins rubric at the Praises: setting 4 verses and chanting the appointed Resurrection stichera of Tone 4. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1114]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4: first Resurrection sticheron, glorifying Christ the Almighty Lord Who endured the Cross and death, and arose from the dead. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1115]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4: second Resurrection sticheron, commemorating the deliverance from the ancient curse and the abolition of the devil through Christ's death and Rising. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1116]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4: third Resurrection sticheron, entreating the Risen Lord to guide us into His truth and deliver us from the snares of the adversary. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1117]: Stichera at the Praises, Tone 4: fourth Resurrection sticheron (Eastern / Anatolian), confessing the only-begotten Word of God Who without parting from the Father's bosom became man and granted immortality unto mankind. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1118]: Stichera at the Praises: Doxastikon, Tone 6 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*), depicting Jesus the Fountain of life at Jacob's well leading the Samaritan woman to proclaim Him as God Incarnate (cf. Jn 4:5–29). Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1119]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs" (Ꙗ҆́кѡ до́блѧ): first sticheron, celebrating the Samaritan woman receiving freely the healing water of life from the Word. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1120]: Psalm verse accompanying the second Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 44:5 [LXX 44:5 / MT 45:4]: "Bend Thy bow, and proceed prosperously, and reign, for the sake of truth, and meekness, and righteousness." Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1121]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs": second sticheron, Christ overcoming the woman's objections regarding Jewish separation to grant her the divine living water. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1122]: Psalm verse accompanying the third Aposticha sticheron: Psalm 44:8 [LXX 44:8 / MT 45:7]: "Thou hast loved righteousness, and hated iniquity." Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1123]: Stichera at the Aposticha, Tone 4, Podoben: "As one valiant among martyrs": third sticheron, the Samaritan woman questioning Christ concerning His lack of a vessel, Whom He led to confess Him as God of all creation. Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1124]: Stichera at the Aposticha: Doxastikon, Tone 6 (*Сла́ва, и҆ ны́нѣ*), recalling the words of the Lord unto the Samaritan woman concerning the living water of eternal life (Jn 4:10). Physical Leaf p431 (printed fol. 425r).

[^1125]: Four-Ode Canon (Tetraodion) of Saturday of the Samaritan Woman, attributed to St. Joseph the Hymnographer. Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). The acrostic in Greek verse is ᾌδω μέλος τέταρτον ("I sing the fourth song"), transcribed in Slavonic as ᾌдѡ ме́лос те́тартон, сі́есть: Пою̀ пѣ́нїе четве́ртое.

[^1126]: Saturday Tetraodion, Tone 4, Ode 6: Heirmos (*Пожрꙋ́ ти со гла́сомъ хвале́нїѧ Гдⷭ҇и*), derived from Jonah 2:10 [LXX 2:10], the Church offering the sacrifice of praise cleansed by the blood from Christ's side. Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v).

[^1127]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 6: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial ᾌ (Slavonic А). Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). Christ stretching forth His palms upon the Cross, crucifying the spiritual Amalek and delivering the world from bondage.

[^1128]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 6: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial δ (Slavonic Д). Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). Christ shattering hades and raising the imprisoned captives on the third day by His divine power.

[^1129]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 6: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ω (Slavonic Ѡ). Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). The Angel announcing unto the Myrrh-bearing women that the Lord Whom they seek is risen.

[^1130]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 6: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial μ (Slavonic М), completing ᾌδω μ-. Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). Consolation unto the Virgin Mother that her Son has arisen from the tomb as God.

[^1131]: Saturday Tetraodion, Tone 4, Ode 7: Heirmos (*Спасы́й во ѻ҆гнѝ А҆враа́мскїѧ твоѧ̑ ѻ҆́троки*), from Daniel 3:26–56 [LXX], praising the God of the fathers Who delivered the three Hebrew children in the Babylonian furnace. Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v).

[^1132]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 7: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial έ (Slavonic Є). Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). Christ suspended between two thieves at Golgotha, abolishing the dominion of darkness.

[^1133]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 7: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial λ (Slavonic Л). Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). Christ pierced in the side with a lance, pouring forth the ever-flowing stream of His divine blood.

[^1134]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 7: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ο (Slavonic О). Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). The Angel in radiant raiment asking the women why they seek the living among the dead.

[^1135]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 7: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ς (Slavonic С), completing -έλος. Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v). The all-pure Virgin rejoicing to see her Son risen in glory from the tomb.

[^1136]: Saturday Tetraodion, Tone 4, Ode 8: Heirmos (*Рꙋ́цѣ распросте́ръ Данїи́лъ*), from Daniel 6:16–23 and Daniel 3:57–88 [LXX], Daniel shutting the lions' mouths and the youth quenching the fiery furnace. Physical Leaf p432 (printed fol. 426v).

[^1137]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 8: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial τ (Slavonic Т). Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). Christ healing wounded humanity by His own wounds and bestowing immortality through His death.

[^1138]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 8: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial έ (Slavonic Є). Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). Summoning all earthly and heavenly beings to rejoice in the Giver of life Who is risen from the tomb.

[^1139]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 8: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial τ (Slavonic Т). Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). Christ binding the tormentor by His descent and resurrecting all the dead.

[^1140]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 8: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial α (Slavonic А), completing τέτα-. Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). The incarnation of the Word from the Virgin Mary to reverse Adam's fall in Eden.

[^1141]: Saturday Tetraodion, Tone 4, Ode 9: Heirmos (*Є҆́vа ᲂу҆́бѡ недꙋ́гомъ преслꙋша́нїѧ*), contrasting Eve's disobedience bringing the curse with the Virgin's childbearing blossoming blessing upon the world. Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r).

[^1142]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 9: first troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial ρ (Slavonic Р). Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). The cosmic portents at Christ's voluntary crucifixion: the rending of the veil and the darkening of the sun (Mt 27:51).

[^1143]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 9: second troparion, bearing Greek acrostic initial τ (Slavonic Т). Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). Christ pierced upon the Tree to heal Adam's ancient wound caused by transgression in Eden.

[^1144]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 9: third troparion (preceded by *Glory*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ο (Slavonic О). Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). The Master Who holds creation in His palm condescending to be numbered among the dead to save mankind from the serpent's sting.

[^1145]: Saturday Tetraodion, Ode 9: Theotokion (preceded by *Both now*), bearing Greek acrostic initial ν (Slavonic Н), concluding the acrostic ᾌδω μέλος τέтартон. Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r). The Mother of God sorrowing at her Son's crucifixion and rejoicing at His third-day Resurrection.

[^1146]: Saturday of the Samaritan Woman, Divine Liturgy liturgical rubrics and Scriptural citations: Prokeimenon and Alleluia of the day; Epistle: Acts pericope 37 [Acts 15:35–41]; Gospel: John pericope 38 [John 10:27–38]; and Communion hymn of the day. Physical Leaf p433 (printed fol. 427r).

[^1147]: Major Part division: Part VI of the 1907 Lviv Pentecostarion, encompassing the liturgical cycle of the Sixth Sunday after Pascha (Sunday of the Blind Man), the Apodosis of Pascha, the Great Feast of the Ascension of our Lord, and the Seventh Sunday after Pascha (Fathers of the First Ecumenical Council). Physical Leaf p434 (printed fol. 428v).

[^1148]: Description of the engraved woodcut on physical leaf p434 (printed fol. 428v): Christ applying clay to the eyes of the man blind from birth, with the disciples witnessing the miracle and the restored man rejoicing.

[^1444]: Doxastikon at *Lord, I have cried*, Tone 8 (preceded by *Glory... Both now...*): extolling Christ's two-fold works of mercy in granting both physical and spiritual sight to the man blind from birth. Physical Leaf p460 (printed p. 454 / fol. 454r).

Physical Leaf p460 (printed p. 454 / fol. 454r).

[^1448]: Custos / catchword (*Та́же,*) linking the conclusion of Paschal Apodosis Vespers to the service continuing on physical leaf p461. Physical Leaf p460 (printed p. 454 / fol. 454r).

Physical Leaf p461 (printed p. 455 / fol. 455r).

[^1450]: Rubric for Small Compline: following *It is truly right* and the Trisagion prayers, chanting the Kontakion of the Blind Man (*With spiritual eyes...*), and for *Glory... [^1451]: Rubric for the Midnight Office on the Apodosis of Pascha: chanted according to custom with the censer; substituting the Resurrection Troparion in Tone 5 (*The Word co-unoriginate*) after the first Trisagion in place of *Behold, the Bridegroom comes*, and the Paschal Kontakion after the second Trisagion in place of *Remember, O Lord, for Thou art good*; concluding with 40 repetitions of *Lord, have mercy* and the dismissal, while omitting the prayer for the departed (*Remember, O Lord, in hope...*). Physical Leaf p461 (printed p. 455 / fol. 455r).

[^1452]: Title and Matins opening rubric for Wednesday of the Sixth Week after Pascha (the Apodosis or Apodosis of Pascha): the priest begins with the censer before the open Royal Doors, proclaiming *Glory to the holy, consubstantial...*, chanting *Christ is risen from the dead...* with the Paschal verses as on the eve; followed by the Superior or Reader reciting the Lesser Doxology (*Glory to God in the highest...*) and the customary Six Psalms. Physical Leaf p461 (printed p. 455 / fol. 455r).

[^1453]: Rubric for *God is the Lord* (Ps. 117:27 LXX) and the Resurrection Troparion in Tone 5 (*The Word co-unoriginate with the Father and the Spirit...*), chanted twice, followed by *Glory... Both now...* and its Theotokion. Physical Leaf p461 (printed p. 455 / fol. 455r).
